⏺ Artifact("happyrobot-workflow-engine.html")
  ⎿  Published ⧉ https://claude.ai/artifact/1YkQqrfG7LJxv8L1kfuaTa

⏺ I published the summary as a Claude artifact: ⧉
  https://claude.ai/artifact/1YkQqrfG7LJxv8L1kfuaTa

  It's a private link, so only you can open it until you share it from the
  page's Share menu.

  The page covers:

  - Thesis: prompt-only AI leaves decisions to the model. HappyRobot puts a
    deterministic workflow layer under every agent, so the workflow sets the
    steps and the agent handles the conversation.
  - Defining workflows: a no-code editor for customers and FDEs, backed by a
    public API and a TypeScript SDK. Workflows are stored as nodes and edges in
    relational tables, and users can create tens of thousands of them.
  - Executor: an orchestrator that holds the graph and decides what's ready to
    run, plus node actors that run one node each. Actors can expand loops at
    runtime.
  - Throttling: the 10,000-item loop example, engine-level rate limits, and
    per-user calls-per-second and concurrency limits. The post says horizontal
    scaling alone can't prevent "system shock."
  - Voice calls: stateful pods handling up to 200 concurrent calls each, with
    calls lasting 30 to 60 minutes. Workflows pause until the call ends,
    coordinated through signals.
  - Tradeoffs, gaps, and direction: the authors' four tradeoffs, the topics the
    post doesn't cover (retries, failure handling, persistence, observability),
    and the stated plan to turn the engine into a distributed workflow
    operations system.

  I read "claude artifact" as an HTML page rather than an editable Claude Docs
  document. If you wanted it as an editable doc instead, I can recreate it that
  way.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The pipeline runs end to end — notably, the counter-based detector (C16)
  overfits, scoring high lift on dev but F1=0 on holdout, and precision looks
  limited by flickering alerts. I'll test a latch/debounce variant before
  finalizing the variant set.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Latching cuts alarm volume ~4x without improving precision, since the real
  ceiling is the 1% of normal hours that always trip at p99 by construction—and
  oddly, the 24h mean covers fewer events than the 6h mean despite expecting
  better separation. I'll check per-event shift timing before settling on H/W
  values.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Every event has the shift across lags 0–47, so a 24h mean at lag 24 should sit
  ~6 sd above normal and catch nearly all of them. Its 62% coverage looks like
  a bug. Digging in.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The p99 threshold sits ~20 V above the normal mean, well beyond the ~7 V noise
  would produce, meaning normal operation includes sustained voltage excursions
  that never triggered a logged failure. I'll dig into what's causing those
  excursions.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ About 500–800 sustained excursions per signal sit inside "normal" operation,
  far from errors and maintenance. Measuring their shape against true
  pre-failure shifts.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I found that ~1,300 normal-operation excursions per signal mimic true
  pre-failure shifts in size and duration, capping detection precision and
  throwing off the p99 threshold. I'll test a robust threshold approach (median
  + z·MAD) to sidestep this contamination.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Robust thresholds make a big difference — median+MAD on a 24h mean catches
  100% of events at ~0.6 alerts/machine/month, versus 53–72% for the percentile
  version. I'll add "robust" as a fourth threshold scope plus p99.99 to the
  sweep and let the dev/holdout protocol pick the best option.

⏺ Update(src/precursors/detectors.py)
  ⎿  Added 17 lines
      198          for m in range(M):
      199              v = f[m, fit[m]]
      200              thr[m] = np.nanpercentile(v, q) if np.isfinite(v).
           any() else np.nan
      201 +    elif norm == "robust":                                    
      202 +        # Per machine: median + z_q * 1.4826 * MAD of normal h
          +ours, where z_q is                                            
      203 +        # the standard-normal quantile of q (p99 -> 2.33, p99.
          +99 -> 3.72).                                                  
      204 +        # Why: "normal" hours contain ~1,300 unlabeled look-al
          +ike excursions per                                            
      205 +        # signal (same size and length as true pre-failure shi
          +fts, but no                                                   
      206 +        # failure follows). They fill the top ~2% of normal ho
          +urs, so an                                                    
      207 +        # empirical p99 lands INSIDE that cluster and misses r
          +eal precursors.                                               
      208 +        # Median and MAD ignore a 2% contamination; the thresh
          +old then reflects                                             
      209 +        # the noise of truly quiet operation.                 
      210 +        from scipy.stats import norm as _N                    
      211 +        z = _N.ppf(q / 100)                                   
      212 +        for m in range(M):                                    
      213 +            v = f[m, fit[m]]                                  
      214 +            v = v[np.isfinite(v)]                             
      215 +            if len(v):                                        
      216 +                med = np.median(v)                            
      217 +                thr[m] = med + z * 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(v
          + - med))                                                      
      218      else:
      219          raise ValueError(norm)
      220      return thr[:, None]

      328      )

      331 +def lookalike_excursions(panel: Panel, cfg: Config, min_len: i
          +nt = 24) -> dict:                                             
      332 +    """How many sustained excursions look exactly like a pre-f
          +ailure shift but                                              
      333 +    are NOT followed by a failure? This bounds the precision A
          +NY signal-based                                               
      334 +    rule can reach.                                           
      335 +                                                              
      336 +    For each component we use the signal whose pre-failure shi
          +ft is largest                                                 
      337 +    (found from the data, not assumed), standardise it per mac
          +hine with normal                                              
      338 +    stats, smooth over 12h, and cut episodes where the smoothe
          +d z exceeds half                                              
      339 +    the typical pre-failure shift. An episode "ends in a failu
          +re" if a failure                                              
      340 +    of that component is logged between 12h before and 24h aft
          +er its end.                                                   
      341 +    """                                                       
      342 +    mk = build_masks(panel, cfg)                              
      343 +    prof = lag_profiles(panel, cfg, lag_max=72)               
      344 +    out = {}                                                  
      345 +    for c in COMPONENTS:                                      
      346 +        # signal with the biggest mean |z| over lags 0..47 for
          + this component                                               
      347 +        s = max(SIGNALS, key=lambda s: abs(np.mean(prof["signa
          +ls"][c][s][:48])))                                            
      348 +        shift = float(np.mean(prof["signals"][c][s][:48]))    
      349 +        x = panel.X[s]                                        
      350 +        mu = np.array([x[m, mk.normal[m]].mean() for m in rang
          +e(panel.M)])[:, None]                                         
      351 +        sd = np.array([x[m, mk.normal[m]].std() for m in range
          +(panel.M)])[:, None]                                          
      352 +        z = np.sign(shift) * (x - mu) / sd                    
      353 +        hi = np.nan_to_num(rolling_mean(z, 12)) > abs(shift) /
          + 2                                                            
      354 +        ev = panel.events[panel.events.comps.map(lambda q: c i
          +n q)]                                                         
      355 +        E = {m: np.sort(g.t.to_numpy()) for m, g in ev.groupby
          +("m")}                                                        
      356 +        L_f, L_n = [], []                                     
      357 +        for m in range(panel.M):                              
      358 +            d = np.diff(np.r_[0, hi[m].astype(int), 0])       
      359 +            st, en = np.flatnonzero(d == 1), np.flatnonzero(d 
          +== -1)                                                        
      360 +            te = E.get(m, np.array([]))                       
      361 +            for a, b in zip(st, en):                          
      362 +                if b - a < min_len:                           
      363 +                    continue                                  
      364 +                hit = ((te >= b - 12) & (te <= b + 24)).any() 
      365 +                (L_f if hit else L_n).append(int(b - a))      
      366 +        bins = list(range(24, 132, 12))                       
      367 +        out[c] = dict(signal=s, shift_z=round(shift, 2), episo
          +des_ending_in_failure=len(L_f),                               
      368 +                      episodes_without_failure=len(L_n),      
      369 +                      precision_ceiling=round(len(L_f) / max(1
          +, len(L_f) + len(L_n)), 3),                                   
      370 +                      len_hist_failure=np.histogram(L_f, bins=
          +bins)[0].tolist(),                                            
      371 +                      len_hist_none=np.histogram(L_n, bins=bin
          +s)[0].tolist(), bins=bins)                                    
      372 +    return out                                                
      373 +                                                              
      374 +                                                              
      375  def lag_profiles(panel: Panel, cfg: Config, lag_max: int = 168
           ) -> dict:
      376      """Event-triggered averages: what each signal does in the 
           hours before each
      377      component's failures, as a z-score vs that machine's norma
           l operation."""

⏺ I found two flaws in my shortlist logic: the P90 error threshold effectively
  excludes all but one component, and judging redundancy by caught events
  unfairly penalizes detectors that fire earlier. I'll add P80 to the sweep and
  switch to a "same alarm" redundancy measure (Jaccard of firing hours ≥ 0.8),
  reporting unique catches and lead time per detector.

⏺ The quick mode's empirical p-value floor (0.01) sits above the Bonferroni
  threshold, causing everything to be rejected—I'll fix this by falling back to
  the z-score's normal-tail p-value when the floor is hit, then move on to
  writing the report builder.

      578 +def golden_summary(R: dict) -> dict:                          
      579 +    """Which implementation approach wins, decided on DEV and 
          +checked on HOLDOUT.                                           
      580 +                                                              
      581 +    Two questions, answered separately:                       
      582 +      * HOW to turn a signal into an alarm (raw vs smoothed vs
          + CUSUM; global vs                                             
      583 +        per-model vs per-machine vs robust threshold): vote ac
          +ross the five                                                 
      584 +        event kinds using each kind's matched telemetry concep
          +t, dev F1 only.                                               
      585 +      * WHICH precursor to trust most: the top-ranked kept det
          +ector per kind.                                               
      586 +    """                                                       
      587 +    from collections import Counter                           
      588 +                                                              
      589 +    votes, per_kind = Counter(), {}                           
      590 +    for e, r in R["kinds"].items():                           
      591 +        tab = r["approach"]["table"]                          
      592 +        best = max(tab, key=lambda t: t["dev"]["f1"])         
      593 +        key = f"{best['transform']} · {best['norm']}"         
      594 +        votes[key] += 1                                       
      595 +        # how much better than the brief's own example rule (r
          +aw, k=3, global)?                                             
      596 +        ref = [t for t in tab if t["transform"] == "raw (k=3)"
          + and t["norm"] == "global"]                                   
      597 +        top = r["kept"][0] if r["kept"] else None             
      598 +        topc = next((c for c in r["concepts"] if c["cid"] == t
          +op), None)                                                    
      599 +        per_kind[e] = dict(                                   
      600 +            approach=key, dev_f1=best["dev"]["f1"], hold_f1=be
          +st["hold"]["f1"],                                             
      601 +            hold_ci=best["hold_ci"]["f1"], pct=best["pct"],   
      602 +            brief_example_hold_f1=ref[0]["hold"]["f1"] if ref 
          +else None,                                                    
      603 +            top_detector=top, top_title=topc["title"] if topc 
          +else None,                                                    
      604 +            top_rule=topc["rule"] if topc else None,          
      605 +            top_hold=topc["hold"] if topc else None)          
      606 +    return dict(approach_votes=dict(votes), winner=votes.most_
          +common(1)[0][0], per_kind=per_kind)                           
      607 +                                                              
      608 +                                                              
      609  # ============================================================
           =========================
      610  # Everything
      611  # ============================================================
           =========================

          +); if(!b) return; show(b.dataset.tab); };                     
      196 +  const c=R.config;                                           
      197 +  $("subtitle").textContent = `H = ${c.H} h · W = ${c.W} h · r
          +ecovery ${c.recovery_h} h · ${R.dev_machines.length} dev / ${R
          +.hold_machines.length} holdout machines · seed ${c.seed}`;    
      198 +}                                                             
      199 +function show(tab){                                           
      200 +  TAB=tab;                                                    
      201 +  [...$("tabs").children].forEach(b=>b.setAttribute("aria-sele
          +cted", b.dataset.tab===tab));                                 
      202 +  TABS.forEach(([k])=>{ $("t-"+k).hidden = k!==tab; });       
      203 +  if (dirty.has(tab)){ RENDER[tab](); dirty.delete(tab); }    
      204 +  try{ localStorage.setItem("tab",tab); localStorage.setItem("
          +ev",EV);}catch(e){}                                           
      205 +}

      207 +// ===========================================================

      208 +// SUMMARY                                                    
      209 +// ===========================================================
          +=====================                                         
      210 +function rSummary(){                                          
      211 +  const k=K(), g=R.golden, gk=g.per_kind[EV], ks=kept();      
      212 +  const top=ks[0];                                            
      213 +  const look=R.lookalikes;                                    
      214 +  const lk = EV==="any" ? null : look[EV];                    
      215 +  const longKept = R.long[EV].kept;                           
      216 +  const lp = onsetLag();                                      
      217 +  $("t-summary").innerHTML = `                                
      218 +  <div><p class="eyebrow">Summary · ${EVLABEL[EV]}</p>        
      219 +  <h2>${top ? esc(top.title) + " is the strongest precursor" :
          + "No detector survived the gates"}</h2>                       
      220 +  <p class="lede">${k.n_evaluable} evaluable events (${k.n_dev
          +_events} on dev machines, ${k.n_hold_events} on holdout). ${k.
          +n_variants} detector variants from ${k.n_concepts} candidate i
          +deas were tried; everything was chosen on dev machines and eve
          +ry number below is from the untouched holdout machines.</p></d
          +iv>                                                           
      221 +  ${top ? `<div class="card hero">                            
      222 +    <p class="eyebrow">Top detector · ${top.cid}</p>          
      223 +    <div class="rule" style="margin:8px 0 14px">${esc(top.rule
          +)}</div>                                                      
      224 +    <div class="facts">                                       
      225 +      <div class="fact"><b>${pc(top.hold.coverage,0)}</b><span
          +>coverage: events with an alert in their window ${ci(top.hold_
          +ci.coverage)}</span></div>                                    
      226 +      <div class="fact"><b>${pc(top.hold.precision,0)}</b><spa
          +n>precision: alerts followed by an event within H+W ${ci(top.h
          +old_ci.precision)}</span></div>                               
      227 +      <div class="fact"><b>${nf(top.hold.lift,1)}×</b><span>li
          +ft: firing rate before events vs. normal ${ci(top.hold_ci.lift
          +,x=>nf(x,1))}</span></div>                                    
      228 +      <div class="fact"><b>${nf(top.hold.alerts_per_machine_mo
          +nth,2)}</b><span>alerts per machine per month (alarm load)</sp
          +an></div>                                                     
      229 +      <div class="fact"><b>${top.lead_median==null?"–":top.lea
          +d_median+" h"}</b><span>median warning before the event (earli
          +est alert)</span></div>                                       
      230 +      <div class="fact"><b>${pc(top.hold.base_precision,1)}</b
          +><span>precision of a random alert (base rate to beat)</span><
          +/div>                                                         
      231 +    </div></div>` : ""}                                       
      232 +  <div class="card"><h3>Shortlist (kept detectors, holdout)</h
          +3>                                                            
      233 +  ${table([["",r=>`<b>${r.cid}</b>`],["Precursor",r=>esc(r.tit
          +le)],["Rule",r=>`<span class="mono" style="font-size:12px">${e
          +sc(r.rule)}</span>`],                                         
      234 +      ["Lift",r=>nf(r.hold.lift,1)+"× "+ci(r.hold_ci.lift,x=>n
          +f(x,1)),"n"],["Precision",r=>pc(r.hold.precision)+" "+ci(r.hol
          +d_ci.precision),"n"],                                         
      235 +      ["Coverage",r=>pc(r.hold.coverage)+" "+ci(r.hold_ci.cove
          +rage),"n"],["Lead",r=>r.lead_median==null?"–":r.lead_median+" 
          +h","n"],                                                      
      236 +      ["Only it catches",r=>r.unique_hold_catches==null?"–":`$
          +{r.unique_hold_catches} of ${r.hold_catches}`,"n"]], ks)}     
      237 +  ${k.union ? `<p class="note" style="margin-top:10px">All kep
          +t detectors together (alert if any fires): coverage ${pc(k.uni
          +on.hold.coverage)}, precision ${pc(k.union.hold.precision)}, $
          +{nf(k.union.hold.alerts_per_machine_month,2)} alerts per machi
          +ne-month.</p>`:""}                                            
      238 +  </div>                                                      
      239 +  <div class="grid2">                                         
      240 +    <div class="card"><h3>What we found</h3><ul class="tight">
      241 +      <li><b>The trail is short and sharp.</b> Every component
          +'s signature signal moves by 1.3–2.3 standard deviations about
          + <b>${lp} hours</b> before the failure and stays there until i
          +t. Nothing shows up earlier. At H = 72 h, W = 168 h ${longKept
          +.length? `only ${longKept.join(", ")} passed the gates` : "<b>
          +no detector</b> passed the gates"}.</li>                      
      242 +      <li><b>Look-alike excursions cap precision.</b> Excursio
          +ns of the same size and length happen ~${Math.round(avg(Object
          +.values(look).map(v=>v.episodes_without_failure)))} times per 
          +component <i>without</i> a failure following. Any signal-only 
          +rule can reach at most ~${pc(avg(Object.values(look).map(v=>v.
          +precision_ceiling)),0)} precision.</li>                       
      243 +      <li><b>Error codes separate real from look-alike.</b> ${
          +pc(avg(Object.values(look).map(v=>v.share_with_error_failure))
          +,0)} of real pre-failure excursions contain an error code, aga
          +inst ${pc(avg(Object.values(look).map(v=>v.share_with_error_no
          +ne)),0)} of look-alikes. That raises the ceiling to ~${pc(avg(
          +Object.values(look).map(v=>v.precision_ceiling_with_error)),0)
          +} and is why "drift confirmed by an error" ranks first.</li>  
      244 +      <li><b>Approach:</b> the winning way to turn a signal in
          +to an alarm is <b>${esc(g.winner)}</b> (best on dev for ${g.ap
          +proach_votes[g.winner]} of 5 event types). See "Which approach
          + wins".</li>                                                  
      245 +    </ul></div>                                               
      246 +    <div class="card"><h3>How confident are we?</h3>          
      247 +      <p>There are enough events for tight intervals. The hold
          +out 95% interval on the top detector's coverage is ${top?ci(to
          +p.hold_ci.coverage):"–"} and on precision ${top?ci(top.hold_ci
          +.precision):"–"}. Intervals come from a <b>cluster bootstrap o
          +ver machines</b>, so events clustering on one machine are not 
          +treated as independent. Every kept detector beats a circular-s
          +hift null by a wide margin (z ≥ ${top?nf(Math.min(...ks.map(r=
          +>r.null_z)),0):"–"}) after Bonferroni correction over ${k.n_co
          +ncepts} ideas.</p>                                            
      248 +      <p>The bigger uncertainty is <b>external validity</b>. T
          +his dataset is synthetic: shifts start at almost exactly 48 h,
          + and error codes land at almost exactly 24 h, stamped at 06:00
          +. Real lines will be noisier, so treat the absolute numbers as
          + optimistic. The <i>method</i> and the <i>ranking of approache
          +s</i> should transfer better than the numbers.</p>            
      249 +      <p class="note">Smallest event type: comp3, ${R.kinds.co
          +mp3.n_hold_events} holdout events. Its intervals are the wides
          +t; switch the event at the top to see them.</p>               
      250 +    </div>                                                    
      251 +  </div>`;                                                    
      252 +}                                                             
      253 +const avg = a => a.reduce((s,x)=>s+x,0)/a.length;             
      254 +function onsetLag(){                                          
      255 +  // first lag (going back in time) where every component's si
          +gnature drops below 0.5 sd                                    
      256 +  const L=R.lag_profiles, comps=["comp1","comp2","comp3","comp
          +4"]; let worst=0;                                             
      257 +  for (const c of comps){ const s=R.lookalikes[c].signal, z=L.
          +signals[c][s].map(Math.abs);                                  
      258 +    let i=0; while(i<z.length && z[i]>0.5) i++; worst=Math.max
          +(worst,i); }                                                  
      259 +  return worst;                                               
      260 +}

      332 +let PCOMP="comp1", HWDET=null, HWMET="f1";                    
      333 +function rParams(){                                           
      334 +  const c=R.config, G=R.hw_grid; HWDET = HWDET || Object.keys(
          +G.detectors)[0];                                              
      335 +  if (EV!=="any") PCOMP=EV;                                   
      336 +  const lk=R.long[EV];                                        
      337 +  $("t-params").innerHTML = `                                 
      338 +  <div><p class="eyebrow">Parameters</p><h2>Why H = ${c.H} h a
          +nd W = ${c.W} h</h2>                                          
      339 +  <p class="lede">The scoring window for each event covers lag
          +s ${Math.max(c.H,1)}–${c.H+c.W-1} h before it. The event hour 
          +itself is never scored, because the repair record and counter 
          +resets already "know" about the failure.</p></div>            
      340 +  <div class="grid2">                                         
      341 +    <div class="card"><h3>H = ${c.H} h: set by how events are 
          +timestamped</h3>                                              
      342 +      <p>${R.profile.event_hour_counts[6]} of ${R.profile.even
          +ts} events and every maintenance record are stamped at exactly
          + 06:00, so the log is a daily batch. A failure stamped 06:00 m
          +ay really have happened at any point in the previous 24 h. A w
          +indow that ends less than 24 h before the stamp can therefore 
          +contain data from <i>after</i> the real failure. That is leaka
          +ge, and a warning in that span may not be a warning at all.</p
          +>                                                             
      343 +      <p>H = 24 h is the smallest lead time that is a guarante
          +ed warning. It is also one maintenance day, so the crew can ac
          +t on it.</p></div>                                            
      344 +    <div class="card"><h3>W = ${c.W} h: set by where the precu
          +rsor lives</h3>                                               
      345 +      <p>The event-triggered averages below show each signatur
          +e shift running from ~${onsetLag()} h before the failure right
          + up to it. With H = 24, the part of the shift we may still use
          + is lags 24–47, which is W = 24 h. A longer W adds normal-look
          +ing hours (lower lift, same coverage). A shorter W throws away
          + usable warning.</p>                                          
      346 +      <p>The heatmap confirms this on <b>dev machines with unt
          +uned default rules</b>, so W was not tuned on the data used fo
          +r grading.</p></div>                                          
      347 +  </div>                                                      
      348 +  <div class="card"><h3>What the signals do before each failur
          +e</h3>                                                        
      349 +    <div class="inline-ctl"><label class="note" for="pcomp">Co
          +mponent</label>                                               
      350 +      <select id="pcomp">${["comp1","comp2","comp3","comp4"].m
          +ap(x=>`<option ${x===PCOMP?"selected":""}>${x}</option>`).join
          +("")}</select>                                                
      351 +      <span class="note">Mean z-score versus each machine's ow
          +n normal operation, across every failure of this component. Th
          +e shaded band is the scored window.</span></div>              
      352 +    <div class="grid2"><div id="p-lag" class="plot"></div><div
          + id="p-lagerr" class="plot"></div></div>                      
      353 +  </div>                                                      
      354 +  <div class="card"><h3>H × W grid (dev machines, any failure,
          + untuned default rules)</h3>                                  
      355 +    <div class="inline-ctl">                                  
      356 +      <select id="hwdet">${Object.keys(G.detectors).map(n=>`<o
          +ption ${n===HWDET?"selected":""}>${esc(n)}</option>`).join("")
          +}</select>                                                    
      357 +      <select id="hwmet">${["f1","coverage","precision","lift"
          +].map(m=>`<option ${m===HWMET?"selected":""}>${m}</option>`).j
          +oin("")}</select>                                             
      358 +      <span class="note">The outlined cell is the chosen (H, W
          +). Columns with H &lt; 24 can look better only by scoring hour
          +s that may follow the real failure.</span></div>              
      359 +    <div id="p-hw" class="plot tall"></div></div>             
      360 +  <div class="card"><h3>Second horizon: is there anything days
          + ahead? (H = ${lk.H} h, W = ${lk.W} h)</h3>                   
      361 +    <p>The brief notes that some precursors show up days ahead
          +. We re-ran the full protocol on lags ${lk.H}–${lk.H+lk.W-1} h
          +. ${lk.kept.length?`Kept: ${lk.kept.join(", ")}.`:"<b>Nothing 
          +passed the gates.</b>"} The table shows each idea's best varia
          +nt on dev, then its holdout result.</p>                       
      362 +    ${table([["",r=>`<b>${r.cid}</b>`],["Idea",r=>esc(r.title)
          +],["Dev F1",r=>nf(r.dev.f1,3),"n"],["Holdout F1",r=>nf(r.hold.
          +f1,3),"n"],["Holdout lift",r=>nf(r.hold.lift,2),"n"],["Holdout
          + coverage",r=>pc(r.hold.coverage),"n"],["Null z",r=>nf(r.null_
          +z,1),"n"],["Verdict",r=>pill(r.status)+" <span class='note'>"+
          +esc(r.reason)+"</span>"]], lk.concepts)}                      
      363 +  </div>`;                                                    
      364 +  $("pcomp").onchange=e=>{PCOMP=e.target.value; drawLag();};  
      365 +  $("hwdet").onchange=e=>{HWDET=e.target.value; drawHW();};   
      366 +  $("hwmet").onchange=e=>{HWMET=e.target.value; drawHW();};   
      367 +  drawLag(); drawHW();                                        
      368 +}                                                             
      369 +function drawLag(){                                           
      370 +  const L=R.lag_profiles, C=pal(), c=R.config, lags=L.lags;   
      371 +  const band={type:"rect",xref:"x",yref:"paper",x0:Math.max(c.
          +H,1),x1:c.H+c.W-1,y0:0,y1:1,fillcolor:css("--accent-soft"),lin
          +e:{width:0},layer:"below"};                                   
      372 +  plot("p-lag",["volt","rotate","pressure","vibration"].map((s
          +,i)=>({type:"scatter",mode:"lines",name:s,x:lags,y:L.signals[P
          +COMP][s],line:{color:C[i],width:2}})),                        
      373 +    {shapes:[band],xaxis:{title:{text:"hours before the failur
          +e"},autorange:"reversed"},yaxis:{title:{text:"z vs. own normal
          +"}},title:{text:"Telemetry",font:{size:13}}});                
      374 +  plot("p-lagerr",["error1","error2","error3","error4","error5
          +"].map((e,i)=>({type:"scatter",mode:"lines",name:e,x:lags,y:L.
          +errors[PCOMP][e].map(v=>v/(L.errors_normal[e]||1e-9)),line:{co
          +lor:C[i%6],width:1.6}})),                                     
      375 +    {shapes:[band],xaxis:{title:{text:"hours before the failur
          +e"},autorange:"reversed"},yaxis:{title:{text:"error rate ÷ nor
          +mal rate"},type:"log"},title:{text:"Error log",font:{size:13}}
          +});                                                           
      376 +}                                                             
      377 +function drawHW(){

      379 +  const i=G.H.indexOf(c.H), j=G.W.indexOf(c.W);               
      380 +  const shapes = (i>=0&&j>=0)? [{type:"rect",x0:j-.5,x1:j+.5,y
          +0:i-.5,y1:i+.5,line:{color:css("--fg"),width:2.5}}] : [];     
      381 +  shapes.push({type:"rect",x0:-.5,x1:G.W.length-.5,y0:-.5,y1:G
          +.H.indexOf(24)-.5,fillcolor:css("--bg"),opacity:.35,line:{widt
          +h:0}});                                                       
      382 +  plot("p-hw",[{type:"heatmap",z:d,x:G.W.map(String),y:G.H.map
          +(String),colorscale:[[0,css("--sunk")],[1,css("--accent")]],  
      383 +      text:d.map(r=>r.map(v=>HWMET==="lift"?nf(v,1):nf(v,2))),

          +.3f}<extra></extra>",colorbar:{thickness:10}}],               
      384 +    {shapes,xaxis:{title:{text:"W (hours)"},type:"category"},y

          +ef?`, against ${nf(brief.hold.f1,3)} for the brief's example r

          +<div id="p-curves" class="plot tall"></div></div>             
      435 +  </div>

          +heir own normal-operation score, the same rule every detector 
          +follows.</p><div id="p-fam" class="plot tall"></div></div>    
      438 +    <div class="card"><h3>What the ML model leans on</h3><p cl
          +ass="note">Permutation importance of the gradient-boosting mod

          +y the signal, and that counters and machine age add little.</p

          +ew Set(A.table.map(t=>t.transform))];

          +.find(t=>t.transform===tr&&t.norm===nm));

          +:null),marker:{color:C[i]},                                   
      444 +      error_y:{type:"data",symmetric:false,array:rows.map(r=>r
          +?r.hold_ci.f1[1]-r.hold.f1:0),arrayminus:rows.map(r=>r?r.hold.
          +f1-r.hold_ci.f1[0]:0),color:css("--muted"),thickness:1},      
      445 +      customdata:rows.map(r=>r?[r.pct,r.dev.f1]:[null,null]),h
          +overtemplate:`${nm} · %{x}<br>holdout F1 %{y:.3f}<br>dev F1 %{
          +customdata[1]:.3f} · P%{customdata[0]}<extra></extra>`};});   
      446 +  const devm=norms.map((nm,i)=>{const rows=trs.map(tr=>A.table
          +.find(t=>t.transform===tr&&t.norm===nm));                     
      447 +    return {type:"scatter",mode:"markers",showlegend:false,x:t
          +rs,y:rows.map(r=>r?r.dev.f1:null),marker:{symbol:"diamond",siz
          +e:7,color:css("--fg")},offsetgroup:nm,hoverinfo:"skip",xaxis:"
          +x",yaxis:"y"};});                                             
      448 +  plot("p-appr",[...data],{barmode:"group",yaxis:{title:{text:
          +"F1 (holdout)"},rangemode:"tozero"},xaxis:{title:{text:"signal
          + transform"}}});                                              
      449 +  // overlay dev diamonds by computing offsets                
      450 +  Plotly.addTraces("p-appr", norms.map((nm,i)=>{const rows=trs
          +.map(tr=>A.table.find(t=>t.transform===tr&&t.norm===nm));     
      451 +     return {type:"scatter",mode:"markers",name:i===0?"dev F1"
          +:"",showlegend:i===0,x:trs.map((_,j)=>j+(i-1.5)*0.2),y:rows.ma

          +H,1),x1:c.H+c.W-1,y0:0,y1:1,fillcolor:css("--accent-soft"),lin

      455 +  const P=A.lag_profile, keys=Object.keys(P);                 
      456 +  plot("p-why",[...keys.map((t,i)=>({type:"scatter",mode:"line
          +s",name:t,x:P[t].by_lag.map((_,j)=>j),y:P[t].by_lag,line:{colo

          +nd:false,x:[0,120],y:[P[t].normal,P[t].normal],line:{color:C[i
          +],dash:"dot",width:1},hoverinfo:"skip"}))],                   
      458 +     {shapes:[band],xaxis:{title:{text:"hours before the event
          +"},autorange:"reversed"},yaxis:{title:{text:"share of events w
          +ith detector ON"},tickformat:".0%"}});                        
      459 +  const cv=A.curves;                                          
      460 +  plot("p-curves",Object.entries(cv).map(([n,v])=>{const tr=n.
          +split(" · ")[0], i=trs.indexOf(tr);                           
      461 +     return {type:"scatter",mode:"lines+markers",name:n,x:v.co
          +verage,y:v.precision,text:v.pct.map(p=>"P"+p),line:{color:C[(i

          +10}}});

          +ncept].includes(r.cid))) fam.push([`${r.cid} ${r.title}`,r.hol
          +d.f1,r.hold_ci.f1]);                                          
      466 +  fam.push([`${A.concept} first draft (${mc.default_rule.lengt

          +2]?f[2][1]-f[1]:0),arrayminus:uniq.map(f=>f[2]?f[1]-f[2][0]:0)
          +,color:css("--muted"),thickness:1},hovertemplate:"%{y}: F1 %{x
          +:.3f}<extra></extra>"}],                                      
      471 +     {xaxis:{title:{text:"F1 (holdout)"},rangemode:"tozero"},m
          +argin:{l:260,t:10}});                                         
      472 +  const imp=Object.entries(R.ml[`${EV}:hgb`].importance).slice
          +(0,12).reverse();                                             
      473 +  plot("p-imp",[{type:"bar",orientation:"h",y:imp.map(x=>x[0])
          +,x:imp.map(x=>x[1]),marker:{color:css("--accent")}}],         
      474 +     {xaxis:{title:{text:"drop in average precision when shuff
          +led"}},margin:{l:190,t:10}});                                 
      475 +}                                                             
      476 +function whyText(best){                                       
      477 +  const t=best.transform, n=best.norm; const bits=[];         
      478 +  if (t.startsWith("roll")) bits.push(`Averaging over ${t.slic
          +e(4)} h shrinks hourly noise by about √${t.slice(4)} while a s
          +ustained shift keeps its size, so the shift stands out. Raw re
          +adings either fire on noise (low precision) or need extreme th
          +resholds (low coverage).`);                                   
      479 +  if (t.startsWith("raw (k")) bits.push("Requiring consecutive
          + readings filters single spikes, but the hourly noise is so la
          +rge that the run rarely completes inside the window.");       
      480 +  if (t==="cusum") bits.push("CUSUM accumulates evidence of a 
          +sustained shift, but resets slowly after the look-alike excurs
          +ions and so raises extra alarms.");                           
      481 +  if (n==="robust") bits.push("A robust threshold (median + z·
          +MAD of each machine's normal hours) beats percentile threshold
          +s because ~2% of \"normal\" hours are unlabeled look-alike exc
          +ursions. An empirical P99 lands inside that cluster and misses
          + real precursors; median and MAD ignore it.");                
      482 +  if (n==="machine") bits.push("A per-machine threshold adapts
          + to each machine's own baseline.");                           
      483 +  if (n==="global"||n==="model") bits.push("Machines share alm
          +ost the same baselines here, so a fleet-wide threshold loses l
          +ittle.");                                                     
      484 +  return bits.join(" ");                                      
      485 +}                                                             
      486 +

          +=====================                                         
      488 +// AUDIT                                                      
      489 +// ===========================================================
          +=====================                                         
      490 +function rAudit(){                                            
      491 +  const A=R.audits, ks=kept("any"), k=R.kinds.any;            
      492 +  const lk=A.lucky;                                           
      493 +  $("t-audit").innerHTML = `                                  
      494 +  <div><p class="eyebrow">Audit · any failure</p><h2>Trying to
          + break our own results</h2>                                   
      495 +  <p class="lede">Each check targets one trap the brief names:
          + leakage, lucky picks from many tries, machine mode changes, c
          +lustered events, and fragile window edges. Audits run on the "
          +any failure" shortlist (${ks.map(r=>r.cid).join(", ")}).</p></
          +div>                                                          
      496 +  <div class="grid2">                                         
      497 +    <div class="card"><h3>1 · Is it luck? Circular-shift null<
          +/h3><p class="note">Each machine's alert series is rotated by 
          +a random offset of at least one week, ${R.config.n_null_shifts
          +} times. This keeps how often and how burstily the detector fi
          +res, but destroys its alignment with failures. Bars show the n
          +ull distribution of dev F1; the line is the real score.</p><di
          +v id="a-null" class="plot tall"></div></div>                  
      498 +    <div class="card"><h3>2 · Would a careless search find "pr
          +ecursors" in pure noise?</h3><p class="note">${lk.dev_f1.lengt
          +h} detectors built on random noise, tuned the same way. The be
          +st one on dev scores F1 ${nf(lk.best_dev,3)}; on holdout it sc
          +ores ${nf(lk.best_hold,3)}. Our kept detectors (green) sit far
          + outside the noise cloud on both axes, so their scores are not
          + a selection effect.</p><div id="a-lucky" class="plot tall"></
          +div></div>                                                    
      499 +  </div>                                                      
      500 +  <div class="grid2">                                         
      501 +    <div class="card"><h3>3 · Does dev performance hold on hol
          +dout?</h3><p class="note">Each point is one candidate's tuned 
          +variant. Points on the diagonal generalise. Points far below i
          +t were overfitted to dev machines.</p><div id="a-dvh" class="p
          +lot tall"></div></div>                                        
      502 +    <div class="card"><h3>4 · Fragile window edges?</h3><p cla
          +ss="note">Holdout coverage as H moves by ±2 h around ${R.confi
          +g.H} h, with W fixed. Error codes land at exactly 24 h before 
          +failures (both stamped at 06:00). A detector that relies on th
          +em sits on the window edge and collapses once H passes 24 h. D

          +son)]], rej)}                                                 
      564 +  </div>                                                      
      565 +  <div class="grid2">                                         
      566 +    <div class="card"><h3>Main design choices</h3><ul class="t
          +ight">                                                        
      567 +      <li><b>Event:</b> ${EV==="any"?"any component failure; f
          +ailures on the same machine at the same timestamp are one even
          +t":"failures involving "+EV} (switch at the top; all five are 
          +computed). Configurable with <span class="mono">--event</span>
          +.</li>                                                        
      568 +      <li><b>H = ${c.H} h:</b> events are stamped once a day a
          +t 06:00, so only ≥ 24 h of lead is a guaranteed warning. Confi
          +gurable with <span class="mono">--H</span>.</li>              
      569 +      <li><b>W = ${c.W} h:</b> the signature shift spans the l
          +ast ~48 h, so after H = 24 h exactly 24 h of it is usable. Con
          +firmed on the dev-only H×W grid. Configurable with <span class
          +="mono">--W</span>.</li>                                      
      570 +      <li><b>Window:</b> lags [max(H,1), H+W). The event hour 
          +is never scored.</li>                                         
      571 +      <li><b>Normal operation:</b> not within H+W h before <i>
          +any</i> failure (even when targeting one component), not withi
          +n ${c.recovery_h} h after any failure or maintenance, not in t
          +he first ${c.warmup_h} h of warm-up. Machines never stop, so e
          +very other hour counts as running.</li>                       
      572 +      <li><b>Thresholds:</b> always fitted on normal hours onl
          +y. Four scopes compete: global and per-model (fitted on dev ma
          +chines), per-machine percentile, and robust per-machine (media
          +n + z·MAD). Percentiles P95–P99.99; for error counts P80–P99.5
          + (P80 means "≥ 1 error").</li>                                
      573 +      <li><b>Selection metric:</b> F1 of precision and coverag
          +e. It rewards neither a never-firing detector (high lift) nor 
          +an always-on one (high coverage). Lift is a gate (≥ 2), not th
          +e target.</li>                                                
      574 +      <li><b>Approach:</b> overall winner <b>${esc(g.winner)}<
          +/b>.</li>                                                     
      575 +    </ul></div>                                               
      576 +    <div class="card"><h3>Assumptions</h3><ul class="tight">  
      577 +      <li>Failure timestamps are a daily batch, so a failure h
          +appened within 24 h before its stamp. Errors are stamped the s
          +ame way.</li>                                                 
      578 +      <li>The repair logged with each failure is part of the f
          +ailure, not a precursor.</li>                                 
      579 +      <li>Telemetry is a true hourly reading at the stamped ho
          +ur. It is causal: every feature uses only past hours.</li>    
      580 +      <li>Machines run continuously; there is no idle mode to 
          +exclude.</li>                                                 
      581 +      <li>Machines are exchangeable enough that dev machines i
          +nform holdout machines. The split is stratified by model.</li>
      582 +      <li>Alerts in the ${c.recovery_h} h after a repair would
          + be muted in practice, so they are not counted.</li>          
      583 +      <li>The look-alike excursions without failures are real 
          +behaviour of this data (possibly averted failures or simulator
          + noise). We do not try to relabel them.</li>                  
      584 +    </ul></div>                                               
      585 +  </div>

⏺ I'm auditing the results before polishing further, and something looks off:
  C16, a maintenance-counter feature for comp1/comp3/comp4, is the sole survivor
  at the long horizon despite scoring F1 = 0 on holdout in an earlier trial.
  I'll dig into why it's persisting.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ C16 holds up on holdout but its huge lift looks like leakage—the "normal"
  baseline excludes pre-failure hours, so the threshold may just be encoding
  proximity to maintenance, plus it peeks at future intervals. I'll test whether
  it survives a strictly causal Jan–Jun threshold and a threshold fit on all
  hours without event-based exclusion.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ C16 turns out to be leakage, not a true precursor—its apparent skill vanishes
  once thresholds are fit only on past data, since excluding pre-failure hours
  simply encoded where failures occur. I'm adding a fourth protocol gate that
  refits thresholds on Jan–Jun and scores Jul–Dec, rejecting any candidate whose
  F1 drops more than half versus full-year thresholds.

      296 -    <p>A live reading moves up and down at random. A counter c
          -limbs by exactly +1 each hour and then resets. Telemetry conta
          -ins no counters. The maintenance log produces one ("hours sinc
          -e last maintenance"), and summing errors would produce another
          -. Counters need care: their normal distribution is set by the 
          -maintenance schedule, so a percentile threshold on them encode
          -s "older than this machine usually gets". The audit shows the 
          -result: lift in the hundreds but precision near zero on holdou
          -t.</p>                                                        
      296 +    <p>A live reading moves up and down at random. A counter c
          +limbs by exactly +1 each hour and then resets. Telemetry conta
          +ins no counters. The maintenance log produces one ("hours sinc
          +e last maintenance"), and summing errors would produce another
          +. Counters need care, because their "normal" distribution depe
          +nds on where the failures are. Normal operation excludes the h
          +ours just before each failure, and those are exactly the hours
          + where a maintenance counter reaches its highest values. A per
          +-machine P99.99 threshold on normal hours therefore means "lon
          +ger than any service interval that did <i>not</i> end in failu
          +re". That uses the machine's whole year, future included. The 
          +"overdue maintenance" candidates show the result: lift ~1,800 
          +with steady holdout F1, which collapses to ~0 once the thresho
          +ld may use only past data. The causal gate rejects them. A rea
          +l but weak age effect remains: intervals that end in failure h
          +ave a median of 75 days, against 30 for scheduled ones.</p>

          + F1 moves from ${nf(a.hold.f1,3)} to ${nf(b.hold.f1,3)}.`;    
      482 +  return s;                                                   
      483 +}                                                             
      484  function whyText(best){
      485    const t=best.transform, n=best.norm; const bits=[];
      486    if (t.startsWith("roll")) bits.push(`Averaging over ${t.slic
           e(4)} h shrinks hourly noise by about √${t.slice(4)} while a s
           ustained shift keeps its size, so the shift stands out. Raw re
           adings either fire on noise (low precision) or need extreme th
           resholds (low coverage).`);

      454  # H/W grid (dev only, untuned default rules to avoid circulari

      455  # ============================================================

      456  def hw_grid(panel, bank, base: Config, dev, detectors: dict) -
           > dict:
      457 -    res = {name: {m: np.full((len(H_GRID), len(W_GRID)), np.na
          -n) for m in ("f1", "lift", "precision", "coverage")}

      457 +    """Metrics over an H x W grid (dev machines, untuned defau
          +lt rules).                                                    
      458 +                                                              
      459 +    Caution, learned the hard way: precision and coverage are 
          +NOT comparable                                                
      460 +    across W. A longer window makes "an event follows within H
          ++W" easier for                                                
      461 +    any alarm (the random-alarm precision rises with H+W) and 
          +gives each event                                              
      462 +    more hours to be caught, so F1 rises with W by constructio
          +n. Lift moves the                                             
      463 +    other way (dilution). We therefore also store precision di
          +vided by the                                                  
      464 +    random-alarm base rate, and choose W from the signal's phy
          +sics, not from                                                
      465 +    a single metric.                                          
      466 +    """                                                       
      467 +    mets = ("f1", "lift", "precision", "coverage", "base_preci
          +sion", "precision_over_random")                               
      468 +    res = {name: {m: np.full((len(H_GRID), len(W_GRID)), np.na
          +n) for m in mets} for name in detectors}                      
      469      for i, H in enumerate(H_GRID):
      470          for j, W in enumerate(W_GRID):
      471              mk = build_masks(panel, base.with_(H=H, W=W))
      472              for name, det in detectors.items():
      473                  s = summarize(machine_stats(fire(det, bank, mk
           .normal, dev), mk)[dev])
      474 +                s["precision_over_random"] = s["precision"] / 
          +s["base_precision"]                                           
      475                  for m in res[name]:
      476                      res[name][m][i, j] = s[m]
      477      return dict(H=list(H_GRID), W=list(W_GRID),

  confidence intervals, while the real robust findings are that 6h mean
  transforms win everywhere on dev and CUSUM is consistently worst, with the
  biggest gain coming from combining drift with error codes. I'll rewrite the
  README and report verdict to reflect this.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've done Option 1 end to end in its own folder,
  /Users/avijay/Documents/oden_precursors/, and the interactive report is open
  in your browser (outputs/report.html). It has 7 tabs (Summary, Dataset, H & W,
  Candidates, Which approach wins, Audit, Write-up) and a switch at the top to
  change the event between any failure and comp1–comp4. Everything is in Python,
  and every number on the page comes from the results file.

  I checked it in headless Chrome: all 35 tab and event combinations render with
  no errors. Notebook 02 reproduces the pipeline's holdout numbers exactly.

  How it was tested. I split the 100 machines 50/50. Every choice was made on
  the first half, and every reported number comes from the other 50 machines. I
  tried 755–775 detector variants built from 17 candidate ideas. To be kept, a
  detector had to beat a shuffled-timing null test (corrected for testing many
  ideas) and still work when its thresholds were set from past data only.

  Main results for "any failure" (unseen machines; an alert fired at random 
  would be right 4% of the time):

  ┌─────────────────────────┬───────────┬──────────┬──────┬─────────────────┐
  │      Kept detector      │ Precision │ Coverage │ Lift │     Warning     │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────┼──────┼─────────────────┤
  │ Signal drift confirmed  │ 60%       │ 100%     │ 8.4× │ 24 h ahead      │
  │ by an error code        │           │          │      │                 │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────┼──────┼─────────────────┤
  │ Any error code          │ 64%       │ 45%      │ 7.9× │ 24 h ahead      │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────┼──────┼─────────────────┤
  │ Any signal drift        │ 28%       │ 97%      │ 8.8× │ 44 h ahead      │
  │                         │           │          │      │ (earliest)      │
  └─────────────────────────┴───────────┴──────────┴──────┴─────────────────┘

  What I found:
  - The warning signs are short. Each part has one signal that shifts about 48 h
    before it fails (comp1 voltage, comp2 rotation, comp3 pressure, comp4
    vibration), and error codes arrive exactly 24 h before. At 3–10 days ahead,
    nothing passes for "any failure".
  - Signals alone can't be precise. About 470 shifts per part look identical but
    have no failure after them, so a signal-only rule tops out at roughly
    21–34% precision. 96–100% of real pre-failure shifts come with an error
    code, against 14–18% of the look-alikes. That's why "drift confirmed by an
    error" wins.
  - Which approach is best, honestly. A 6-hour average was the best way to
    smooth the signals for all 5 event types. But its lead over the brief's own
    example rule is small and within the margin of error, and for comp3 and
    comp4 it's slightly behind. The differences that matter are bigger: one
    method (CUSUM) is clearly worst, and adding the error-code confirmation
    gains about 0.3 F1.
  - Simple rules beat machine learning here (F1 0.75 against 0.57).

  Corrections I made along the way (each is listed in the README, since the
  brief asks where the AI needed correcting):
  - A detector that passed every statistical test was rejected. "Hours since
    last maintenance" passed everything, but it only worked because of how
    "normal" operation is defined: it was quietly using future information. With
    past-only thresholds its score fell to near zero, so I added the past-only
    check as a rule for every candidate.
  - My first H & W page said the evidence confirmed a 24-hour window. It didn't.
    Longer windows make precision look better even for random alerts (4% at 24
    h vs 16% at 168 h). The window size is now justified by when the signals
    actually shift, not by a score.

  Settings I chose:
  - Lead time 24 hours. Failures are logged once a day at 06:00, so anything
    shorter might not be a real warning.
  - Window 24 hours. It covers the usable part of the 48-hour shift.
  - Normal operation excludes the period before any failure and 24 hours after
    repairs.

  All of these can be changed with flags on run_pipeline.py, or edited in
  src/precursors/config.py.

  Before you submit:
  - The brief asks for an export of the AI conversation; that export has to come
    from you.
  - This goes well beyond the brief's suggested 4 hours, so you may want to trim
    it.
  - The data is synthetic and unusually clean, so treat the absolute numbers as
    optimistic. The method and the ranking of approaches should hold up better
    than the numbers.

  Files are in /Users/avijay/Documents/oden_precursors/ (re-run everything with
  run_pipeline.py):
  - README.md
  - outputs/report.html
  - notebooks/01_data_profile.ipynb
  - notebooks/02_precursor_search.ipynb
  - notebooks/03_audit.ipynb

❯ did you consult prior kaggle submissions to see what the "best" ones did?     

⏺ No, I didn't look at any Kaggle notebooks or prior submissions. Everything
  came from the data plus what I already knew about this dataset. I should have
  said that in the README's AI-usage notes, and I can add it.

  What I knew going in: this dataset comes from Microsoft's Azure
  predictive-maintenance template, and the common Kaggle approach copies it:

  - Features: 3h and 24h rolling means and standard deviations of the four
    signals, error counts over the last 24h, days since each component was last
    replaced, machine model and age.
  - Label: "fails within the next 24h", usually predicted by one
    gradient-boosting model across all components.
  - Reported scores: often 95–99%+ accuracy, usually from random row splits (the
    same machine in train and test, with overlapping windows) and class
    imbalance that makes accuracy look good.

  Here is how my work compares:

  - Same direction: rolling means beat raw readings, and error counts matter.
    Both match the common approach, and my sweep reached them independently.
  - Different on purpose: the brief wants simple fixed rules with lift,
    precision and coverage, not one classifier. So I used ML only as a benchmark
    and to generate ideas.
  - The interesting overlap is "days since last replacement". It's usually one
    of the top features in those notebooks, and it's the counter my causal check
    rejected. In my setup it leaked through how "normal" was defined. My own ML
    benchmark scored worse on unseen machines when it used it. Prior notebooks
    may be getting part of their high scores from this leak, but I haven't
    verified that.

  If you want, I can review the most-voted notebooks properly and add a short
  "How this compares to prior Kaggle work" section to the report and README. It
  would cover what they did, where my results agree, and whether their strong
  numbers hold under a machine-level split. It would cost maybe 20–30 minutes
  and would make the "honest statistics" point to Oden concrete.

⏺ I've pinned down the canonical approach: labels via 21h backfill on a 3h grid,
  features from 3h/24h telemetry stats, error counts, and maintenance recency,
  with a time-split GradientBoosting model hitting ~0.98 macro F1. The key
  insight is their model pairs components with specific signals (e.g., comp1 ↔
  volt + error1), so I'll test whether matched component-signal pairs outperform
  my current "any drift + any error" rule (C17).

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Requiring a matched error-component pair (not just any error) boosts
  drift-alert precision to 0.82–0.88 across components, and I've narrowed the
  real pairs to a clean set via a ≥50%-of-max-enrichment rule. Next, I'll add
  this as candidate C18, replicate the canonical model with controlled
  one-change-at-a-time variations (window, counter, split type), and align
  scoring methods so the two approaches can be compared fairly.

      613 +def learn_mapping(panel: Panel, cfg: Config, dev) -> dict:    
      614 +    """Which signal and which error codes belong to which comp
          +onent - learned                                               
      615 +    on DEV machines only, so C18 never sees holdout labels.   
      616 +                                                              
      617 +    signal: the one with the largest mean |z| over the 48h bef
          +ore that                                                      
      618 +            component's failures (z vs each machine's normal o
          +peration)                                                     
      619 +    errors: codes whose rate in the 48h before the failures is
          + at least half                                                
      620 +            as enriched (vs normal) as the most enriched code.
          + (A fixed                                                     
      621 +            cut-off of 5x was tried first and wrongly pulled i
          +n error5 for                                                  
      622 +            comp2/comp3 - a side effect of multi-part failures
          +.)                                                            
      623 +    """                                                       
      624 +    mk = build_masks(panel, cfg)                              
      625 +    ev = panel.events[panel.events.m.isin(dev)]               
      626 +    lags = np.arange(48)                                      
      627 +    out = {}                                                  
      628 +    for c in COMPONENTS:                                      
      629 +        e = ev[ev.comps.map(lambda q: c in q)]

      633 +        best, bz = None, 0.0

          +range(panel.M)])                                              
      637 +            sd = np.array([x[m, mk.normal[m]].std() for m in r
          +ange(panel.M)])                                               
      638 +            z = np.where(ok, (x[mm, np.clip(tt, 0, None)] - mu
          +[mm]) / sd[mm], np.nan)                                       
      639 +            mz = float(np.nanmean(z))                         
      640 +            if abs(mz) > abs(bz):                             
      641 +                best, bz = s, mz                              
      642 +        enr = {}                                              
      643 +        for k, er in enumerate(ERRORS):                       
      644 +            x = panel.err[:, :, k]

          + 0, None)], np.nan))

          +e 0.0

           operator cost model to choose the threshold.</li>
      616        <li>Repeat the dev/holdout split over many seeds to show
            how stable the selection is.</li>
      617        <li>Try a dataset with minute-level data and noisier, re
           al timestamps; this one is synthetic and unusually clean.</li>

⏺ Not yet. Both jobs started only about 5 minutes ago and are still running. The

  Already changed in the code, based on the prior-work review:

  1. New precursor, C18 "matched signature". It alerts when a part's own signal
     drifts and its own error code is logged within 48 h. This came from the
     canonical Kaggle approach (Microsoft's Predictive Maintenance Modelling
     Guide), which uses component-specific features. Which signal and which
     codes belong to which part is learned from the dev machines only
     (volt+error1 → comp1, rotate+error2/3 → comp2, pressure+error4 → comp3,
     vibration+error5 → comp4). In a trial run on the unseen machines it was the
     clear winner:
     - "Any failure": F1 0.95 (90% precision, 100% coverage, 0.7 alerts per
       machine per month), against 0.75 for my previous best rule.
     - comp1 and comp3: F1 0.95 and 0.93, against 0.39 and 0.32 for single
       signals.
     - Caveat: like every error-based rule, it fires exactly 24 h before the
       failure, right on the edge of my H = 24 window. The report will say so.
  2. Replication of the published model. Their exact model on my rebuild of
     their features scores macro F1 0.988, against 0.980 published, so the
     rebuild is faithful. Now running: five one-change-at-a-time variants (no
     counters, my 24–45 h horizon, both, split by machine), plus my C18 rule
     scored with their metric.
  3. New "Prior work" tab, which already reads both results files:
     - their recipe, with sources;
     - a replication chart;
     - what the stress tests show;
     - a table of what I adopted and what I didn't, with reasons.

  Corrections I made along the way:
  - My first stand-in for their model (a faster gradient-boosting variant)
    scored 0.85 instead of 0.98 and then diverged when tuned. I switched to
    their exact model and recorded why in the code.
  - My first rule for learning the pairings picked up spurious error codes. I
    replaced it.

⏺ Both background jobs are still running (about 20 minutes in). The notebooks

  Notebook comments (done in the builder): every code cell now comments its
  lines with what it computes, why it's needed, and what to look for in the
  output. Each notebook opens with a "how to read this" guide, and notebook 01
  ends with a summary table answering each item the brief asks about.

  Yes, there is a "goodness" metric. It works in three layers:

  1. The brief's three numbers, each shown on its own:
     - Lift: does it fire more before failures than in normal operation?
     - Precision: when it alerts, does a failure follow?
     - Coverage: what share of failures got an alert?

     Each one alone can be gamed. A detector that almost never fires gets a huge
     lift, and one that's always on gets 100% coverage. So none of them is the
     target by itself.
  2. The single score for "good": F1 of precision and coverage, their harmonic
     mean. It is high only when most alarms are real and most failures are
     caught, which is what an operator wants. Each idea keeps its best-F1
     variant on the dev machines, and the shortlist is ranked by it.
  3. Pass/fail gates before anything counts. A detector must:
     - beat a shuffled-timing null (corrected for testing many ideas),
     - reach lift ≥ 2,
     - reach coverage ≥ 5%,
     - keep at least half its F1 when thresholds may only use past data.

  Also reported alongside F1:
  - The precision a random alarm would get (about 4% here); that is the bar to
    clear.
  - Alerts per machine per month (alarm load).
  - Median lead time.
  - 95% intervals from resampling machines, all on the 50 holdout machines.

  One honest limitation: F1 weighs a missed failure and a false alarm equally.
  In a real plant the costs differ: a missed breakage may cost far more than an
  unneeded inspection. With a cost per false alarm and per missed failure from
  Oden, the natural upgrade is to pick thresholds by expected cost rather than
  F1. The report notes this as a next step.

  That explanation is now also a "How 'good' is scored" card on the report's
  Summary tab, so readers see it first.

  What the review found. Most Kaggle notebooks on this dataset copy Microsoft's
  Predictive Maintenance Modelling Guide. Kaggle's pages couldn't be read
  directly, so I read the guide's code and saved outputs from a GitHub copy. Its
  results reproduced: 0.988 against the published 0.980.

  New shortlist for "any failure" (holdout machines; a random alarm would be
  right 4% of the time):

  C18 (new): a part's own signal drifts and its own error code is logged
  Precision: 90%
  Coverage: 100%
  F1: 0.95
  Alarm load: 0.7 per machine-month
  Warning: 24 h
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  C17: any signal drifts and any error code is logged (previous best)
  Precision: 60%
  Coverage: 100%
  F1: 0.75
  Alarm load: 1.1
  Warning: 24 h
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  C19 (new): a part's own signal drifts and the part is older than usual since
  replacement
  Precision: 41%
  Coverage: 87%
  F1: 0.55
  Alarm load: 4.1
  Warning: 43 h

  - C18 came from the guide's part-specific features. Which signal and which
    error codes belong to which part is learned from the dev machines only. It
    ranks first for all four parts (F1 0.93–0.99).
  - C19 came from a test that removed one thing at a time from their model. It
    is an early warning: precision rises from 28% (signal drift alone) to 41%.
  - Use them as two tiers. C19 is a watchlist about 2 days ahead; C18 is the
    "act now" alarm 1 day ahead. If the lead time H moves from 24 h to 25 h,
    C18's coverage falls from 100% to 5%, because the error code is logged
    exactly 24 h before the failure. C19 keeps 87%. Relying on error codes alone
    would be fragile.

  What the one-change-at-a-time tests showed (their 3-hourly row score, called
  macro F1, published at 0.98):
  - On their 0–21 h window, the error codes carry the score; dropping the
    maintenance counter barely matters (0.91 vs 0.91).
  - At my 24–45 h window, before the error code exists, the counter carries
    almost everything (0.81 with it vs 0.30 without).
  - In two of the seven fits, one part's score collapsed to near zero while the
    others stayed near 0.97. That looks like instability in their single model,
    not something in the data.

  What I didn't adopt, and why:
  - Their 0–21 h label: it includes the failure hour itself and the last day,
    which the 06:00 daily batch log can't place reliably.
  - Their 3-hourly row metric: 98% of rows are trivially "no failure", and each
    failure counts eight times.

  Two of my earlier claims were wrong, and I've corrected them:
  - The maintenance counter is not leakage in general. It is legitimate inside a
    model trained on earlier months. It only fails as a percentile-of-normal
    rule (C16).
  - My ML benchmark's poor result with counters came from how I defined
    "normal", not from the counter itself.

  Your two other requests:
  - Notebook comments: every code cell is now commented line by line (what it
    does, why, and what to look for), about 45% of code lines, plus a reading
    guide per notebook.
  - "Goodness" metric: shortlisted detectors are ranked by the F1 of precision
    and coverage, after four pass/fail gates. Lift and alarm load are shown
    alongside. This is now a card on the Summary tab and a section in
    notebook 02.

  The report has a new "Prior work" tab with sources and a table of what I took
  and didn't. The README results and its list of AI corrections are updated.

  Still yours to do: export this conversation for the AI-transcript part of the
  submission. The work also goes well past the brief's ~4-hour scope.

❯ how does thed ata get pumped in in 01_notebook?                               

  Listed 1 directory (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ All the data enters notebook 01 through one line in its second code cell:

  panel = load_panel()

  Behind that line, load_panel() in src/precursors/data.py:64 runs four steps:

  1. Find or download the files (data.py:28)
  kagglehub.dataset_download("arnabbiswas1/microsoft-azure-predictive-maintenanc
  e")
  The first time, this downloads the 31 MB dataset from Kaggle (no account
  needed). After that it reuses the cached copy, so it works offline:
  ~/.cache/kagglehub/datasets/arnabbiswas1/microsoft-azure-predictive-maintenanc
  e/versions/3/
  That folder holds the five CSVs: telemetry, errors, maint, failures, machines.

  2. Read the CSVs with pandas (data.py:66–70).

  3. Check the assumptions (data.py:78–80). It asserts there are exactly 100
  machines × 8,761 hours, no gaps in the time axis, and no missing values. If
  any of these fails, the cell stops with an error instead of producing wrong
  results later.

  4. Reshape into analysis-ready arrays (data.py:81–130):
  - Telemetry: one 100 × 8,761 array per signal (volt, rotate, pressure,
    vibration). Row = machine, column = hour.
  - Errors: hourly counts per error code, 100 × 8,761 × 5.
  - Maintenance: which part was serviced each hour, plus "hours since each
    part's last maintenance". It uses records back to mid-2014, so parts have a
    known age on day one.
  - Failures: merged so that the same machine and the same hour count as one
    event (761 rows → 719 events), with each event's machine and hour index.
  - Machines: model and age.

  It returns a single Panel object. Every later cell reads from it:
  panel.X["volt"], panel.events, panel.err, panel.maint_raw, and so on.

  The pipeline, notebook 02 and the replication script all call this same
  load_panel(), so they all see identical data. Notebook 03 is the exception: it
  doesn't load raw data at all, only the saved outputs/results.json.

  To see the raw tables before reshaping, run this in a cell:
  panel.failures_raw.head(), panel.errors_raw.head(), panel.maint_raw.head()

※ recap: You're building the Oden take-home: finding precursors to machine failures, with an interactive HTML report and three runnable notebooks. Next, in VS Code, select the .venv (Python 3.13.3) interpreter, then click Run All on each notebook.

  reliable: if I hand-copy the code into the notebooks, there would be two
  versions of the analysis (notebooks and pipeline) that can quietly drift
  apart. Instead, I'll have the notebook builder copy the code, comments and 
  all, directly from those 7 files into notebook cells, placed step by step with
  a short explanation before each. So:

  - the notebooks import nothing from src/ and read top to bottom as one
    self-contained story;
  - the code you see is exactly the code the pipeline and the HTML report use;
  - notebook 02 ends with an optional cross-check that its live results match

⏺ Done. The notebooks no longer rely on the .py files: all 7 that were used are
  now written out directly inside notebooks 01 and 02.

  How it reads now:
  - Notebook 01 has 5 cells marked "Definition", each placed right before the
    step that first uses it. Settings come before loading the data, "normal
    operation" before the first plot that needs it, and so on.
  - Notebook 02 opens with "0 · Building blocks": seven short steps (settings →
    data → event windows → detector rules → candidates → metrics → selection
    protocol). Each has a plain-language explanation, then its code. The
    analysis follows.
  - Every definition keeps its original comments and explanations. Cells that
    only define code say so, so it's clear which ones compute results.

  Checked:
  - The project code can't even be imported from the environment, and both
    notebooks still run start to finish with no errors. So they don't depend on
    src/ at all.
  - Notebook 02's last cell compares itself with the pipeline: same three kept
    detectors (C18, C17, C19) and identical holdout F1 for all 18 ideas. The
    notebook and the HTML report tell the same story.
  - Notebook 03 already used no .py files. It only reads the saved results and
    checks they're current.

  One trade-off to know about: the code in the notebooks is copied from
  src/precursors/ each time scripts/build_notebooks.py runs. That keeps a single
  source of truth, and the notebooks can't silently drift from what the
  pipeline runs. But if you edit a definition inside a notebook, only that
  notebook changes, not the pipeline or the HTML. To change the analysis
  everywhere, edit the .py file, then re-run run_pipeline.py and
  scripts/build_notebooks.py.

  The notebooks are longer now (01: ~570 code lines, 02: ~1,370), because they
  include everything. In VS Code, use the outline view (Explorer → Outline) to
  jump between sections.
