      163 +        code('''# Distribution of each sensor across all machi
          +nes and hours.                                                
      164 +# Expect roughly bell-shaped readings with long tails: the tai
          +ls are where precursors (and look-alikes) live.               
      165 +long = tel.melt(id_vars=["datetime", "machineID"], value_vars=
          +["volt", "rotate", "pressure", "vibration"])                  
      166 +px.histogram(long, x="value", facet_col="variable", facet_col_
          +wrap=4, nbins=80,                                             
      167 +             title="Sensor readings, all machines and hours").
          +update_xaxes(matches=None).update_yaxes(matches=None)'''),    
      168 +        md("""## 1.2 · Errors: controller fault codes         
      169 +An error is logged while the machine keeps running, so it's a 
          +symptom, not a breakdown."""),                                
      170 +        code('''err = raw["errors"]                           
      171 +display(err.head())                                           
      172 +print("errors per code:", err.errorID.value_counts().sort_inde
          +x().to_dict())                                                
      173 +per_m = err.groupby("machineID").size()                       
      174 +print(f"errors per machine: median {per_m.median():.0f}, range
          + {per_m.min()}-{per_m.max()}")                                
      175 +                                                              
      176 +# How often each code appears per month. A flat line means the
          + error rate is stable over the year.                          
      177 +monthly = err.groupby([err.datetime.dt.to_period("M").astype(s
          +tr), "errorID"]).size().reset_index(name="count")             
      178 +px.line(monthly, x="datetime", y="count", color="errorID", mar
          +kers=True, title="Error codes per month")'''),                
      179 +        md("""## 1.3 · Maintenance: component replacements    
      180 +Includes both **scheduled** replacements and the **repair** lo
          +gged after every failure. It starts in mid-2014,              
      181 +six months before the telemetry. That's useful: every part has
          + a known age from day one."""),                               
      182 +        code('''mnt = raw["maint"]                            
      183 +display(mnt.head())                                           
      184 +print("replacements per component:", mnt.comp.value_counts().s
          +ort_index().to_dict())                                        
      185 +print("date range:", mnt.datetime.min(), "->", mnt.datetime.ma
          +x(),                                                          
      186 +      f"({(mnt.datetime < tel.datetime.min()).sum()} rows befo
          +re telemetry starts)")

          +ict(), " <- all at 06:00: a daily batch log")                 
      188 +

          +e same machine.

          +placements of the same part"),                                
      192 +             title="How long a part runs before it is replaced
          +").update_layout(showlegend=False)'''),                       
      193 +        md("""## 1.4 · Failures: the rare events              
      194 +The events we want precursors for. Each row is one component f
          +ailing on one machine."""),

      196 +display(fail.head())

          +ort_index().to_dict())

          + {fpm.min()}-{fpm.max()}; "                                   
      200 +      f"{(fpm == 0).sum()} machines never fail")              
      201 +print("hour of day:", fail.datetime.dt.hour.value_counts().to_
          +dict())                                                       
      202 +                                                              
      203 +# Same machine + same timestamp = one event (the brief's rule)
          +. How many rows collapse?                                     
      204 +print("rows:", len(fail), "| distinct (machine, timestamp) eve
          +nts:", len(fail.drop_duplicates(["machineID", "datetime"])))  
      205 +                                                              
      206 +# Is every failure also in the maintenance log (the repair)? E
          +xpect almost all.                                             
      207 +both = fail.merge(mnt.rename(columns={"comp": "failure"}), on=
          +["machineID", "datetime", "failure"])                         
      208 +print(f"failures with a matching repair in the maintenance log
          +: {len(both)} of {len(fail)}")                                
      209 +                                                              
      210 +# Every failure on one chart: machine (y) against date (x), co
          +loured by component.

          + opacity=.7,

          +stack",

      219 +        md("""## 1.6 · How the files connect                  
      220 +Every file shares **`machineID`**. The four event-style files 
          +also share **`datetime`**, rounded to the hour,               
      221 +so they line up with the telemetry grid.                      
      222 +                                                              
      223 +| Join | Key | What it gives you |                            
      224 +|---|---|---|                                                 
      225 +| telemetry ↔ machines | `machineID` | each reading's machine 
          +model and age |

          +ime` | which events happened at that hour |                   
      227 +| failures ↔ maint | `machineID` + `datetime` + component | th
          +e repair that goes with each failure |                        
      228 +

          + year."""),                                                   
      230 +        code('''# One machine's year: a sensor trace with its 
          +errors, replacements and failures marked on top.              
      231 +# Pick the machine with the most failures so there is plenty t
          +o see.

      233 +t = tel[tel.machineID == mid].set_index("datetime")           
      234 +day = t.vibration.resample("D").mean()                 # daily
          + mean, so the year fits on one chart                          
      235 +                                                              
      236 +fig = go.Figure()                                             
      237 +fig.add_scatter(x=day.index, y=day.values, name="vibration (da
          +ily mean)", line=dict(color="gray"))                          
      238 +top = day.max() * 1.05                                        
      239 +for df, label, sym, col in ((err[err.machineID == mid], "error
          +", "line-ns", "#b76e00"),

          +cement", "triangle-up", "#0e6170"),

          +lure", "x", "#b03a3a")):

          +rkers", name=label,

          + line=dict(width=1.5, color=col)),                            
      244 +                    hovertext=df.iloc[:, 2].astype(str))   # e
          +rror code / component on hover

          + with its errors, replacements and failures",                 
      246 +                  yaxis_title="vibration", height=420)        
      247 +fig   # look for: errors cluster just before failures; every f
          +ailure has a replacement at the same moment'''),              
      248 +        md("""### What Part 1 tells us                        
      249 +* **Clean and complete:** every machine has a reading every ho
          +ur of 2015, with no missing values.                           
      250 +* **Timestamps are batched:** failures and maintenance are all
          + stamped at 06:00 (a few failures at 03:00),                  
      251 +  so they are logged once a day, not at the exact moment.     
      252 +* **Failures are rare and uneven:** 761 rows over 100 machines
          +, between 0 and ~19 per machine, so a model                   
      253 +  must not treat every machine alike.                         
      254 +* **Failures and repairs are two views of the same moment:** a
          +lmost every failure has a matching replacement                
      255 +  in the maintenance log. Using that replacement as a "precurs
          +or" would be leakage.                                         
      256 +* **Errors are symptoms:** about 3,900 codes over five types, 
          +at a stable rate through the year.                            
      257 +* **The data looks synthetic:** very regular distributions, ro
          +und 06:00 stamps, perfectly complete. Results                 
      258 +  here will be cleaner than on a real production line."""),

  It closes with a short "What Part 1 tells us" summary. I checked every number
  in it against the cells' outputs:
  - Clean and complete: 100 machines, a reading every hour of 2015, no missing
    values.
  - Rare and uneven failures: 761 rows, 0–19 per machine, and 2 machines never
    fail.
  - Stable error rate: about 3,900 error codes, 291–368 per month.
  - Daily batch log: maintenance and failures are stamped at 06:00.
  - New quirk: 743 of 761 failures have a matching repair record. The 18 that
    don't are exactly one odd batch stamped 03:00 on 2 Jan 2015. That's the only
    exception to the 06:00 rule. The notebook now prints this, and the summary
    mentions it.

❯ why do we have prior_work.json, wahts the point of it?                        
⏺ prior_work.json holds the results of rebuilding the most popular public Kaggle
  approach for this dataset and stress-testing it. It's the evidence behind the
  "Prior work" part of the analysis.

  What's in it: the canonical public model's score, rebuilt from Microsoft's
  guide, plus five versions with one thing changed each time:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │               Version               │ Row-level F1 │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ As published                        │ 0.91         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Without the maintenance counter     │ 0.91         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ At our 24–45 h horizon              │ 0.81         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Our horizon, without the counter    │ 0.30         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Split by machine instead of by time │ 0.78         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┘

  It also scores our C18 rule the same way, so the two approaches can be
  compared on the same metric.

  Why it's a saved file: producing it means fitting that slow model 15 times,
  about 30 minutes on 8 cores. That's too slow for a notebook's Run All, so
  src/prior_work.py runs it once and saves the results. Notebook 03 (section 6)
  just reads them.

  Why it matters to the analysis:
  1. It's where two of the three kept detectors came from. The replication
     showed that the popular model's strength comes from part-specific features.
     That led to C18, now the best detector, and C19, the early warning.
  2. It backs claims we make:
     - At their 0–24 h horizon the error codes do the work.
     - At our horizon, part age does.
     - Their headline 0.98 is a per-3-hour-row score that's easy to inflate.

     Without this file, those statements would be assertions.
  3. The brief asks for honest statistics and awareness of common traps. Showing
     that you reproduced the "standard" answer, and found what it does and
     doesn't hold up to, is strong evidence of both.

  Could you drop it? Yes. The brief doesn't require looking at prior work.
  Dropping it would remove src/prior_work.py, prior_work.json and notebook 03's
  section 6, leaving 11 files. The key numbers would stay in the README table.
  C18 and C19 would still exist, because they're defined in src/detectors.py,
  but the evidence for why they were tried would be gone.

  My recommendation is to keep it. It's one helper file and one data file, and
  it tells the strongest part of the story: you checked the popular answer,
  learned from it, and improved on it.

❯ keep it, doe sthis mean that their model si ebtter than ours?                 

⏺ Not overall. They're built for different jobs and scored differently, and one
  part of the comparison favours them. Here's what the evidence does and doesn't
  show.

  Where their model is better:
  - At their own task, labelling every 3-hour row in the last day before a
    failure, their model scores 0.91, against 0.76 for our C18 rule scored the
    same way. That's expected: a model trained on 30 features for exactly that
    task should beat a two-condition rule at it.
  - More importantly, at our early horizon (24–45 h ahead), their model still
    scores 0.81 when it can use part age. Our rules can't match that row by row.
    This is a real signal. Combining telemetry with "how long since this part
    was replaced" holds more early warning than our simple rules capture. Our
    early-warning rule C19 uses that idea, but only reaches 41% precision.

  Where the comparison isn't fair to either side:
  - Different metric. Their score counts each 3-hour row separately. A failure
    caught 1 day ahead with one alert counts as 1 of 8 rows. That's why C18
    scores only 0.28 on their metric at our horizon, even though, measured the
    brief's way, it catches 100% of failures with 90% of alerts being real.
  - Different horizon. Their headline 0.98 includes the final 24 hours before
    the failure stamp. Given the daily 06:00 batch log, that window may contain
    data from after the real failure.
  - Different brief. Oden asked for simple fixed rules an engineer can read and
    trust, not the best classifier. Their model is a 100-tree black box.

  What we haven't measured: their model on our terms. That means treating its
  predictions as alerts and computing lift, alert precision and event coverage
  on the unseen machines at H = 24. That's the one comparison that would settle
  "is it better at what Oden asked for?". I'd expect it to beat C19 as an early
  warning, but probably not C18 at 1 day ahead, which is already near the
  ceiling (90% precision, 100% coverage).

  How I'd say it to Oden: "Our C18 rule matches what a full model achieves at a
  one-day lead, with two conditions an engineer can check by hand. The prior
  work shows a model using part age can warn earlier than our rules can. That's
  the clearest next step."

  If you'd like, I can run that head-to-head once the current run finishes:
  train their exact model on the dev machines and score it as alerts on the
  holdout machines, alongside C18 and C19. It would take about 15 minutes of
  compute and add one table to notebook 03's prior-work section.

❯ what is the C18 rule exactly                                                  

⏺ Here's C18 as it was selected for "any failure" (src/detectors.py §5; tuned on
  the dev machines).

  The rule. Alert on a machine when, for any one of its four parts, both of
  these are true at the same hour:

  ┌───────┬────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Part  │ Its own signal drifts… │ and its own error code was logged │
  ├───────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp1 │ voltage above normal   │ error1 in the last 24 h           │
  ├───────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp2 │ rotation below normal  │ error2 or error3 in the last 24 h │
  ├───────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp3 │ pressure above normal  │ error4 in the last 24 h           │
  ├───────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp4 │ vibration above normal │ error5 in the last 24 h           │
  └───────┴────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  "Drifts" precisely: the signal's 24-hour trailing average (this hour and the
  23 before it, never future hours) is more than 3.72 robust standard deviations
  away from that machine's median during normal operation:

  threshold = median(normal hours) ± 3.72 × 1.4826 × MAD(normal hours)    # 
  computed per machine

  - MAD is the median absolute deviation. × 1.4826 turns it into a
    standard-deviation equivalent that isn't inflated by outliers. That matters
    here because "normal" hours contain the look-alike excursions.
  - 3.72 is the normal-distribution point for P99.99. A quiet machine almost
    never gets there by chance.
  - "Normal operation" means hours that are not within 48 h before any failure,
    not within 24 h after a repair, and not in the first day of data.

  "Error logged" precisely: the count of that part's error codes in the last 24
  h is above the 80th percentile of that count in normal operation. That
  percentile is 0, because most normal 24-hour stretches have none of a given
  code. So in plain terms: at least one of the part's own error codes was
  logged.

  The pairing: which signal and which codes belong to which part was learned
  from the dev machines only, by checking which signal shifts and which codes
  become more frequent before each part's failures. Holdout machines played no
  part.

  When it fires: an alert is raised each time the rule switches from off to on.
  In this data, the matching error code is logged exactly 24 h before the
  failure, while the signal has already been drifting for about a day. So C18
  almost always fires 24 h before the failure stamp.

  How it performs on the 50 unseen machines (350 failures):

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┬────────────┐
  │                                      │       Value        │    95%     │
  │                                      │                    │  interval  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Coverage (failures with an alert in  │ 100%               │ [100, 100] │
  │ the window)                          │                    │            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Precision (alerts followed by a      │ 90%                │ [87, 93]   │
  │ failure within 48 h)                 │                    │            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Lift                                 │ 32×                │ [24, 48]   │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Alarm load                           │ 0.7 per machine    │            │
  │                                      │ per month          │            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Precision of a random alarm, for     │ 4.1%               │            │
  │ comparison                           │                    │            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┴────────────┘

  Why both conditions are needed:
  - Drift alone: about 470 look-alike drifts per part never end in failure, so
    precision is ~28%.
  - Any error code alone: errors happen randomly too, so precision is ~64% and
    only 45% of failures are caught.
  - Both together, for the same part: the look-alikes rarely come with their own
    part's error code (5–11% do), while real failures almost always do
    (96–100%). That's what lifts precision to 90%.

  Its weakness: it depends on the error arriving exactly 24 h before the
  failure. Move the window edge by one hour (H = 25) and coverage drops to 5%.
  That's why C19, drift on an aging part, sits alongside it as an earlier,
  independent warning.

❯ how is goodness being measured?                                               

⏺ Goodness is measured in three layers. Each is in notebook 03 (section 2
  explains it, section 3 applies it) and in the code at src/evaluation.py §6–§7.

  1. The brief's three numbers, all reported:

  ┌───────────┬──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │  Metric   │           Question           │ Why it can't be the target   │
  │           │                              │          on its own          │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │           │ Does it fire more in the 24  │ A detector that almost never │
  │ Lift      │ h window before failures     │  fires can get a huge lift   │
  │           │ than in normal operation?    │ from one lucky hit           │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │           │ When it raises an alert      │ A detector that fires only   │
  │ Precision │ (switches off → on), is a    │ on the most obvious cases    │
  │           │ failure logged within the    │ looks precise but misses     │
  │           │ next 48 h?                   │ most failures                │
  ├───────────┼──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │           │ What share of failures had   │ A detector that is always on │
  │ Coverage  │ at least one alert in their  │  gets 100%                   │
  │           │ window?                      │                              │
  └───────────┴──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  2. One score to rank on: F1 of precision and coverage. That's their harmonic
  mean, 2·P·C / (P + C). It's high only when most alarms are real and most 
  failures are caught, the two things an operator cares about. Each idea keeps
  the variant with the best F1 on the 50 dev machines, and the shortlist is
  ranked by it.

  3. Pass/fail gates before anything can be kept (all on dev machines):
  1. Not luck: each machine's alerts are shifted around in time at random, 500
     times, and the real F1 must clearly beat that. The test is
     Bonferroni-corrected because 18 ideas are tried.
  2. Lift ≥ 2.
  3. Coverage ≥ 5%.
  4. No help from the future: with thresholds fitted on Jan–Jun only, it must
     keep at least half its Jul–Dec F1.

  Shown alongside, for judgement:
  - Random-alarm precision: about 4% here, the bar every detector must clear.
  - Alarm load: alerts per machine per month.
  - Lead time: how early the first alert comes.
  - Honest uncertainty: every number comes from the 50 holdout machines that
    never influenced a choice, with 95% intervals from resampling whole
    machines.

  C18 scores, for example: precision 90% [87, 93], coverage 100%, lift 32×, F1
  0.95, 0.7 alerts per machine-month, 24 h lead.

  One limitation: F1 treats a missed failure and a false alarm as equally bad.
  On a real line a missed breakage usually costs far more than an unneeded
  inspection. With those two costs, the natural upgrade is to choose detectors
  by expected cost instead. It's listed under "What we would do next" in
  notebook 05.

❯ how do my lift, precision coverage compare to prior work (from online)        

⏺ The short answer: there's no like-for-like comparison yet. The public work
  doesn't report lift, alert precision or event coverage at all, so the closest
  I can give you is the table below. It isn't a fair head-to-head.

  What the popular public approach reports (Microsoft's Predictive Maintenance
  Modelling Guide, which most Kaggle notebooks copy; numbers from its saved
  output, split 1):

  ┌────────────────────────────┬──────────────┬───────┬───────┬───────┐
  │        Their metric        │    comp1     │ comp2 │ comp3 │ comp4 │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ Precision (per 3-hour row) │ 0.96         │ 0.99  │ 0.98  │ 0.99  │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ Recall (per 3-hour row)    │ 0.95         │ 1.00  │ 0.96  │ 0.98  │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ Macro F1 (incl. "none")    │ 0.98 overall │       │       │       │
  └────────────────────────────┴──────────────┴───────┴───────┴───────┘

  Ours (C18, holdout machines; these are the brief's metrics):

  ┌────────────────────────┬──────┬───────┬───────┬───────┬─────────┐
  │       Our metric       │ any  │ comp1 │ comp2 │ comp3 │  comp4  │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────┼───────┼───────┼───────┼─────────┤
  │ Precision (per alert)  │ 0.90 │ 0.90  │ 0.87  │ 0.87  │ 0.99    │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────┼───────┼───────┼───────┼─────────┤
  │ Coverage (per failure) │ 1.00 │ 1.00  │ 1.00  │ 1.00  │ 1.00    │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────┼───────┼───────┼───────┼─────────┤
  │ Lift                   │ 32×  │ 121×  │ 76×   │ 150×  │ ~5,500× │
  └────────────────────────┴──────┴───────┴───────┴───────┴─────────┘

  Why those numbers can't be compared directly:

  ┌───────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │               │          Their numbers           │         Ours         │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ What's        │ every 3-hour row; one failure =  │ each alert, and each │
  │ counted       │ 8 rows                           │  failure once        │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ "Precision"   │ share of rows labelled "compN    │ share of alerts      │
  │ means         │ fails soon" that are right       │ followed by a        │
  │               │                                  │ failure              │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ "Recall" vs   │ share of the 8 pre-failure rows  │ share of failures    │
  │ coverage      │ flagged                          │ with at least one    │
  │               │                                  │ alert                │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │               │ 0–21 h before the stamp, which   │ ≥ 24 h before the    │
  │ Lead time     │ includes the final day the 06:00 │ stamp, a guaranteed  │
  │               │  batch log can't place           │ warning              │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Machines      │ same machines, later months      │ 50 machines never    │
  │               │                                  │ used for any choice  │
  ├───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Lift          │ not reported                     │ reported             │
  └───────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  The only apples-to-apples numbers we have come from notebook 04 §6, where I
  rebuilt their model and scored our rule their way (row-level macro F1):

  ┌───────────────────────┬────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │                       │ Their horizon (0–21 h) │ Our horizon (24–45 h) │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Their model (rebuilt) │ 0.91                   │ 0.81                  │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Our C18 rule          │ 0.76                   │ 0.28                  │
  └───────────────────────┴────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  On their own metric their model wins, as you'd expect from a 30-feature model
  trained for exactly that task. Our rule raises one alert per failure, so it
  gets credit for only one row in eight at our horizon.

  The comparison that would actually answer your question is the reverse: score
  their model with our metrics. That means treating its predictions as alerts,
  then computing lift, alert precision and event coverage on the same holdout
  machines at H = 24. I haven't run it. My expectation:
  - C18 is already near the ceiling at 24 h ahead (90% precision, 100%
    coverage), so there's little room for their model to beat it there.
  - Their model would probably beat C19 as an early warning, because part age
    carries real signal at 1–2 days.

  The other public work I found uses different setups and metrics: a Keras
  "fails within 24 h" model, Azure's 7-day label, and a survival-analysis paper.
  I didn't extract comparable numbers from them, and Kaggle's notebook pages
  couldn't be read directly.

  Want me to run that head-to-head? It's about 15 minutes of compute (one fit of
  their model on the dev machines), and it would add one table to notebook 04
  §6 showing their model and C18/C19 side by side on lift, precision and
  coverage.

❯ there may be an argument for a smaller horizon, audit. idelaly it should eb   
  influenced by the HxW grid                                                    

⏺ That's a fair challenge, and the current justification for H = 24 has a weak
  spot. The window starts exactly 24 h before the failure stamp, so the error
  code (logged exactly 24 h before) sits on the window's edge. That's why C18's
  coverage drops from 100% to 5% at H = 25. And H = 24 was set by an argument
  ("the 06:00 stamp might hide up to a day of post-failure data") that I never
  actually tested.

  So I'll audit it properly in two parts:

  1. Test the assumption. If the stamp really hid post-failure hours, the hours
     just before it would look different: signals back to normal, or
     post-failure error bursts. I'll look hour by hour from 24 h before to 24 h
     after the stamp.
  2. Let the grid decide, fairly. Run the full selection protocol on dev 
     machines only at several leads (H = 1, 6, 12, 18, 24). In each case W
     extends the window back to where the precursor starts (~48 h), i.e. W = 48
     − H. Then compare performance, lead time and sensitivity to the window
     edge.

  Running the hour-by-hour check first:

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The audit shows no post-failure data or error bursts before the stamp—each
  part's signal shift and error code occur well before it—so my earlier
  justification for H = 24 doesn't hold, opening the door to shorter leads. I'll
  now let the protocol pick the lead time itself, running full selection on dev
  machines at five lead values with full-shift windows, tracking dev/holdout
  performance, lead time, and edge fragility for each.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The sweep settles it:

  ┌───────────┬─────┬───────┬──────────┬──────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │           │     │       │ Mean dev │  Alert   │   Coverage if the     │
  │ H (lead)  │  W  │ Kept  │  F1 of   │  lead    │  window starts 1 h    │
  │           │     │       │   kept   │  times   │  later (C17 / C18 /   │
  │           │     │       │          │          │         C19)          │
  ├───────────┼─────┼───────┼──────────┼──────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │           │     │ C17   │          │ 24 h /   │                       │
  │ 1         │ 47  │ C18   │ 0.760    │ 23 h /   │ 0.99 / 0.97 / 0.91    │
  │           │     │ C19   │          │ 43 h     │                       │
  ├───────────┼─────┼───────┼──────────┼──────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 6         │ 42  │ same  │ 0.758    │ 24 / 24  │ 0.97 / 1.00 / 0.91    │
  │           │     │       │          │ / 43 h   │                       │
  ├───────────┼─────┼───────┼──────────┼──────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 12        │ 36  │ same  │ 0.752    │ 24 / 24  │ 0.94 / 0.98 / 0.90    │
  │           │     │       │          │ / 43 h   │                       │
  ├───────────┼─────┼───────┼──────────┼──────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 18        │ 30  │ same  │ 0.747    │ 24 / 24  │ 1.00 / 1.00 / 0.89    │
  │           │     │       │          │ / 43 h   │                       │
  ├───────────┼─────┼───────┼──────────┼──────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 24        │ 24  │ same  │ 0.745    │ 24 / 24  │ 0.17 / 0.03 / 0.87    │
  │ (current) │     │       │          │ / 43 h   │                       │
  └───────────┴─────┴───────┴──────────┴──────────┴───────────────────────┘

  What it shows:
  1. The detectors don't depend on H. The same three are kept at every lead, and
     they alert at the same times (C18 24 h ahead, C19 43 h ahead). H only
     changes which alerts count as on time.
  2. Performance barely moves: mean dev F1 ranges from 0.745 to 0.760.
  3. The real difference is fragility. At H = 24 the window starts exactly where
     the error code lands, so a one-hour shift wipes out C18 and C17. Every
     smaller lead is robust.

  Decision: H = 12 h, W = 36 h (window from 12 to 47 h before the stamp). The
  reasoning:
  - The evidence the detectors use sits at known points: the error code at 24 h
    and the drift starting at 48 h. The window should contain both with a safety
    margin, not cut through either. H = 12 puts the error code 12 h inside the
    window.
  - W = 36 reaches back to the start of the drift.
  - It still requires half a day (one maintenance shift) of warning.
  - Dev F1 is within 0.01 of the best lead, and the result is robust to edge
    shifts.

  H = 1–6 would score marginally higher, but would count an alert one hour
  before a failure as success, which isn't useful operationally.

  This changes the default everywhere, so I'll:
  - update the settings and their rationale in src/data.py §1;
  - make the window-edge audit centre on whatever H is chosen (it's currently
    hard-coded to 22–26);
  - put both the hour-by-hour audit and this lead sweep into the notebooks as
    live evidence;
  - re-run the analysis and correct every H = 24 statement in the notebooks and
    README.

      786 +def stamp_alignment(panel: Panel, cfg: Config, mapping: dict, 
          +before: int = 49, after: int = 24) -> dict:                   
      787 +    """Does a failure stamp hide post-failure data? Hour-by-ho
          +ur averages around the stamp.                                 
      788 +                                                              
      789 +    For each part: its own signal (z-score vs each machine's n
          +ormal operation, signed so a                                  
      790 +    precursor is positive) and its own error codes, averaged o
          +ver all its failures, from                                    
      791 +    `after` hours AFTER the stamp to `before` hours before it.
          + If the stamp were logged up to                               
      792 +    a day late, the last hours before it would look post-failu
          +re (signal back to normal, or                                 
      793 +    bursts of errors). Returns {part: DataFrame indexed by lag
          + (negative = after the stamp)}.                               
      794 +    """                                                       
      795 +    mk = build_masks(panel, cfg)                              
      796 +    lags = np.arange(-after, before + 1)                      
      797 +    out = {}                                                  
      798 +    for c, mp in mapping.items():                             
      799 +        x = panel.X[mp["signal"]]                             
      800 +        mu = np.array([x[m, mk.normal[m]].mean() for m in rang
          +e(panel.M)])[:, None]                                         
      801 +        sd = np.array([x[m, mk.normal[m]].std() for m in range
          +(panel.M)])[:, None]                                          
      802 +        z = (1 if mp["direction"] == "high" else -1) * (x - mu
          +) / sd                                                        
      803 +        e = panel.events[panel.events.comps.map(lambda q: c in
          + q)]                                                          
      804 +        e = e[(e.t - before >= 0) & (e.t + after < panel.T)]  
          +        # full span inside the data                           
      805 +        tt = e.t.to_numpy()[:, None] - lags[None, :]          
      806 +        mm = e.m.to_numpy()[:, None]                          
      807 +        own = sum(panel.err[:, :, ERRORS.index(k)] for k in mp
          +["errors"])                                                   
      808 +        out[c] = pd.DataFrame({"own_signal_z": z[mm, tt].mean(
          +0),                                                           
      809 +                               "own_error_codes_per_failure": 
          +own[mm, tt].mean(0),                                          
      810 +                               "any_error_codes_per_failure": 
          +panel.err.sum(axis=2)[mm, tt].mean(0)},                       
      811 +                              index=pd.Index(lags, name="hours
          +_before_stamp"))                                              
      812 +    return out                                                
      813 +                                                              
      814 +                                                              
      815 +def lead_sweep(panel: Panel, bank: FeatureBank, base: Config, 
          +dev, hold, leads=(1, 6, 12, 18, 24),                          
      816 +               reach: int = 48, n_null: int = 100) -> pd.DataF
          +rame:                                                         
      817 +    """Run the full selection protocol at several lead times H
          +, each window reaching back to the                            
      818 +    start of the precursor (W = reach - H, i.e. lags H .. reac
          +h-1). Everything that drives the                              
      819 +    choice of H is measured on DEV machines; holdout F1 is sho
          +wn only for reference.                                        
      820 +                                                              
      821 +    `edge_cov_*` = DEV coverage of each kept detector if the w
          +indow starts one hour later                                   
      822 +    (H+1, same W): a large drop means the window edge cuts thr
          +ough the evidence the detector uses.                          
      823 +    """                                                       
      824 +    rows = []                                                 
      825 +    for H in leads:                                           
      826 +        cfg = base.with_(H=H, W=reach - H)                    
      827 +        r = run_event_kind(panel, cfg, bank, dev, hold, n_null
          +, verbose=False)                                              
      828 +        concepts = {c.cid: c for c in build_concepts(cfg.perce
          +ntiles, cfg.event, MAPPING)}                                  
      829 +        kept = [c for c in r["concepts"] if c["status"] == "ke
          +pt"]                                                          
      830 +        mk1 = build_masks(panel, cfg.with_(H=H + 1))          
      831 +        row = dict(H=H, W=reach - H, kept=" ".join(r["kept"]),
      832 +                   mean_dev_f1_kept=round(float(np.mean([c["de
          +v"]["f1"] for c in kept])), 4))                               
      833 +        for c in kept:                                        
      834 +            d = [v for v in concepts[c["cid"]].variants if v.r
          +ule_text() == c["rule"]][0]                                   
      835 +            row[f"dev_f1_{c['cid']}"] = c["dev"]["f1"]        
      836 +            row[f"lead_h_{c['cid']}"] = c["lead_median"]      
      837 +            row[f"edge_cov_{c['cid']}"] = round(float(summariz
          +e(machine_stats(fire(d, bank, mk1.normal, dev), mk1)[dev])["co
          +verage"]), 3)                                                 
      838 +            row[f"hold_f1_{c['cid']}"] = c["hold"]["f1"]      
      839 +        rows.append(row)                                      
      840 +    return pd.DataFrame(rows)                                 
      841 +

⏺ Taking your guidance on board, the default window is now H = 0, W = 48, chosen
  by F1. The full analysis is re-running with it (~10 more minutes). After that
  I'll execute all five notebooks and check every number against the new
  results.

  How the decision leaned on existing work rather than new machinery:
  - Kaggle convention as the starting point. The popular approach asks "will it
    fail within the next 24 hours?". We ask the same question with 48 hours,
    because notebook 01's lag profiles show the warning signs start 48 h before
    a failure.
  - F1 decides, using the protocol we already had:

  | Window                               | C18 dev F1 | Early-warning C19 |
  |--------------------------------------|------------|-------------------|
  | next 48 h (H = 0, W = 48)            | 0.945      | kept              |
  | next 24 h (Kaggle)                   | 0.936      | drops out         |
  | H = 12, W = 36 (my earlier proposal) | 0.936      | kept              |
  | H = 24, W = 24 (old default)         | 0.930      | kept              |

  - The stamp audit removed the only argument for a lead. The hour-by-hour check
    shows no post-failure data before the 06:00 stamps, so H = 24 was never
    needed to avoid leakage.

  What doesn't change: the same three detectors win, and they still alert as
  early as before (C18 ~24 h ahead, C19 ~43 h ahead). H only decides which
  alerts count as on time.

  What changed in the notebooks (exact passages only):
  - 01: your H/W explainer, with new numbers and diagram; the stamp audit added
    to section 3; section 9's summary.
  - 03: section 6b, which compares the windows by F1 including the Kaggle one,
    plus the grid reading.
  - 04: the window-edge audit, now centred on H = 0 and keeping H = 24/25 to
    show why the old default was fragile.
  - 05: the H and W design choices and the timestamp assumption.

  I dropped my H = 12 proposal. It came from margin-to-the-edge reasoning, which
  F1 doesn't support.

❯ did u adhere to thsi guardrails:  Keep up to three of your                    
  strongest detectors and briefly explain why they beat the others.             

⏺ Yes, in all three places it applies:

  1. At most three are kept. The selection protocol (run_event_kind,
     src/evaluation.py §7) stops at three. For "any failure" it keeps C18, C17
     and C19, and every other event type also gets exactly three (notebook 03,
     section 7).
  2. "Strongest" is defined by F1, your goodness measure. Survivors of the four
     pass/fail gates are ranked by dev F1, and the top three are kept. One
     refinement: a candidate that is essentially the same alarm as one already
     kept (≥ 80% overlap in firing hours) is skipped, so the three are distinct
     detectors rather than near-copies.
  3. "Briefly explain why they beat the others" has its own section: notebook 
     03, section 4, "Why these three beat the others". It has a table of every
     candidate (holdout F1, precision, coverage, lift, lead time, alarm load and
     the protocol's verdict), then a short explanation:
     - why each kept detector wins: C18 is most precise; C17 is the pairing-free
       fallback; C19 gives the earliest warning;
     - why the rest lose, by group: most passed every gate but were weaker;
       single error codes failed the lift gate; the maintenance counter failed
       the past-data check.

     Notebook 05 repeats the shortlist and the rejected-with-reasons table as
     part of the write-up.

  One thing to re-check: that explanation quotes numbers from the old H = 24
  window (e.g. "~90% precision, F1 0.95"). The dev-only trial at the new H = 0,
  W = 48 window keeps the same three detectors, so the reasoning should hold,
  but the quoted figures will shift slightly. When the current run finishes,
  I'll update section 4 and notebook 05 to the new numbers, along with the rest
  of the verification.

❯ give me full C18 ruleset                                                      

⏺ Pulling the exact rule from the saved results, plus the actual threshold
  values, so the ruleset is concrete rather than only described:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Here is C18 exactly as selected for "any failure" at the final window (H = 0,
  W = 48; tuned on dev machines). It's in src/detectors.py §5, and the code and
  error pairings were learned from the dev machines only.

  Alert on a machine at hour t if ANY of these four part-rules is true:

  ┌───────┬───────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
  │ Part  │ Signal condition (6-hour trailing │ AND error condition (last   │
  │       │     average of hours t−5 … t)     │    24 h, hours t−23 … t)    │
  ├───────┼───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp1 │ voltage above its machine's P99   │ ≥ 1 error1 logged           │
  │       │ of normal operation               │                             │
  ├───────┼───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp2 │ rotation below its machine's P1   │ ≥ 1 error2 or error3 logged │
  │       │ of normal operation               │                             │
  ├───────┼───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp3 │ pressure above its machine's P99  │ ≥ 1 error4 logged           │
  │       │ of normal operation               │                             │
  ├───────┼───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp4 │ vibration above its machine's P99 │ ≥ 1 error5 logged           │
  │       │  of normal operation              │                             │
  └───────┴───────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

  The actual thresholds. The signal limit is computed per machine; across the
  100 machines:

  ┌───────────┬────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Signal   │  Typical normal    │   Alarm limit, median [range across    │
  │           │       level        │               machines]                │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ voltage   │ 170.2              │ > 192.0 [188.5 – 196.0]                │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ rotation  │ 449.2              │ < 363.9 [348.3 – 382.1]                │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ pressure  │ 100.1              │ > 126.1 [123.0 – 129.7]                │
  ├───────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ vibration │ 40.1               │ > 50.6 [49.1 – 51.8]                   │
  └───────────┴────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The error condition is formally "24 h count > P80 of normal operation
  (fleet-wide)". That P80 is 0 for every code, so it means at least one of the
  part's own codes.

  Definitions behind it:
  - "Normal operation", the hours used to set the percentiles, excludes:
    - the 48 h before any failure;
    - the 24 h after any failure or repair;
    - the first 24 h of data.
  - Alert = the rule switching from off to on. An alert in the 24 h after a
    repair is muted, and the failure hour itself is never scored.
  - The score counts an alert as correct if a failure is logged within the next
    48 h. A failure counts as caught if at least one firing hour falls in the 48
    h before it.

  How it performs (50 holdout machines, 350 failures):

  ┌───────┬────────────┬────────────┬──────┬────────────────────┬──────────┐
  │  F1   │ Precision  │  Coverage  │ Lift │    Alerts per      │ Median   │
  │       │            │            │      │   machine-month    │   lead   │
  ├───────┼────────────┼────────────┼──────┼────────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 0.942 │ 92% [90,   │ 96% [94,   │ 351× │ 1.6                │ 23 h     │
  │       │ 95]        │ 98]        │      │                    │          │
  └───────┴────────────┴────────────┴──────┴────────────────────┴──────────┘

  What changed from the version I described earlier. That was the old H = 24
  window, where tuning picked a 24 h average with a robust median ± 3.72 MAD-σ
  limit. At the new window, tuning picks a 6 h average with each machine's 
  P99/P1. The idea is the same: a part's own signal drifting while its own error
  code is logged.

  The analysis re-run is still going, about 8 more minutes. These numbers come
  from the identical configuration, so they won't change. Then come the final
  notebook execution and checks.

※ recap: The goal is notebooks covering every part of the Oden brief, with goodness scored by F1 and the window now "fails within 48 hours". The final analysis run is finishing; next I re-run all five notebooks and check their numbers.

❯ does this violate Keep up to three of your                                    
  strongest detectors and briefly explain why they beat the others or no?       

⏺ No. It's within the brief's rules, but it's worth knowing why someone might
  ask.

  Why it doesn't violate the rule:
  1. The brief explicitly allows combined detectors: "A candidate can use one 
     signal or combine several." Its definition of a detector is "a simple, 
     fixed rule that flags when a precursor is present." C18 is exactly that:
     one fixed yes/no rule, firing when the matched-signature pattern is
     present.
  2. It's one precursor, not four. All four parts follow the same pattern: a 
     part's own signal drifts AND its own error code is logged. The four lines
     just name which signal and code belong to which part. "Vibration rises"
     works the same way, as one idea with one threshold per machine.
  3. The others combine signals too. C17 and C19 each combine four signals, and
     so do rejected ideas like C08. C18 isn't a special case.
  4. The count and the explanation are both there. Exactly three are kept (C18,
     C17, C19), ranked by F1. Notebook 03 §4 explains why they beat the others,
     with a full comparison table.

  Where a reviewer could push back: "Isn't an OR of four part-rules really four
  detectors stacked to get around the limit of three?" Two answers, both already
  in the notebooks:
  - Run per part, it's a single pair. For each component event type, C18 is just
    "own signal + own code", one AND. It still ranks first every time: holdout
    F1 0.94 for comp1, 0.95 for comp2, 0.95 for comp3 and 0.99 for comp4
    (notebook 03 §7).
  - The OR is how it handles "any failure". For "any component fails", any one
    of the four parts can be the one failing, so the rule must watch all four.
    C08 and C17 do the same with "any of four signals".

  If you want to remove the ambiguity for a reviewer, I can add one sentence to
  notebook 03 §4 and the write-up: "C18 counts as one detector: one pattern (own
  signal + own error code) applied to each part, as the brief allows ('a 
  candidate can use one signal or combine several'); per component it is a 
  single two-condition rule."

❯ was any ML used?                                                              

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes, ML was used, but never as a detector. All three kept precursors are
  simple fixed rules. ML played three supporting roles:

  1. As an idea generator and a performance ceiling (src/evaluation.py §8).
  Three models were trained on the 50 dev machines to tell "48 h before a
  failure" from "normal operation", using all ~30 features. Their alert
  threshold was P99 of their own normal-operation score, the same rule as every
  detector. Scored on the holdout machines ("any failure"):

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┬────────────┐
  │                   Model                    │ Holdout F1 │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Logistic regression                        │ 0.63       │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Gradient boosting, no maintenance counters │ 0.53       │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Gradient boosting, all features            │ 0.32       │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ C18 (simple rule), for comparison          │ 0.94       │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────┴────────────┘

  Its feature importances confirmed that the smoothed signals and error counts
  carry the signal, which fed the brainstorm. The full model scored worse with
  maintenance counters, which helped expose the counter leak.

  2. To replicate the prior Kaggle work (src/prior_work.py §9). This was the
  popular approach's exact model, a 5-class gradient-boosting classifier, fitted
  15 times to reproduce its published 0.98 and stress-test it. That's where C18
  and C19 came from.

  3. Not ML, though it might sound like it:
  - the part ↔ signal/error pairing is a simple enrichment ratio, computed on
    dev machines;
  - tuning is a search over fixed rule variants;
  - the null test and confidence intervals are standard resampling statistics.

  One gap. The ML benchmark results are computed and saved in results.json, and
  the README reports them, but no notebook displays them. Notebook 02 only
  mentions ML as an idea source. They were shown only in the HTML report, which
  we removed. Since "simple rules beat ML here" is one of the stronger findings,
  I'd add a short section to notebook 03 next to "Which approach wins": the
  table above, the top features each model relied on, and one sentence on why
  the rules win. It reads from the saved results, so it adds no run time. Want
  me to add it?

❯ so we take on a workflow similar to                                           
  https://engineering.oden.io/blog/agentic-root-cause-analysis                  

⏺ Yes, in structure. Our pipeline follows the same five steps as Oden's post,
  but at a smaller scale, and a few steps are lighter or missing. Here's an
  honest side-by-side:

  Oden's workflow: 1. Operating context: line diagrams, process knowledge,
  operator interviews
  Ours: Data profiling and an audit of how failures are timestamped (notebook
  01). There are no operators or plant documents for a public dataset
  Match: ⚠️ partial: context comes from the data only
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Oden's workflow: 2. ML narrows the search: gradient-boosted classifiers,
  feature importances, SHAP
  Ours: A gradient-boosting benchmark with permutation feature importances, and
  the rebuilt Kaggle gradient-boosting model. Both used as an idea source and a
   ceiling, not as the alarm
  Match: ✅ same role, but run alongside the brainstorm rather than first, and
  no
  SHAP
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Oden's workflow: 3. Hypotheses: an LLM agent turns statistical candidates into

  physical stories, each with a testable statistic
  Ours: 19 candidates, each a physical story plus a fixed rule. Written with an
  AI assistant (Claude), domain knowledge, the ML results and prior Kaggle work
  Match: ✅ same output, but not an automated agent loop inside the pipeline
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Oden's workflow: 4. Statistical filter: lift, precision, permutation tests vs
  random nominal windows, masking downtime/startup
  Ours: Lift, precision and coverage; a circular-shift permutation test;
  thresholds from normal running only; recovery periods masked
  Match: ✅ plus extra checks they don't describe: separate holdout machines, a
  past-data-only check, and multiple-testing correction
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Oden's workflow: 5. Root-cause families, playbooks, live alerts, operator 
  feedback loop
  Ours: A shortlist forming one family per part: C18 to act, C19 as early
  warning, C17 as fallback
  Match: ⚠️ partial: no playbooks, deployment or feedback loop, which need a
  real
  plant

  Where we went beyond the post:
  - Coverage, the third metric the brief asks for, which the post doesn't use;
  - holdout machines never used for any choice, where the post holds out recent
    weeks;
  - the causal check, which caught the maintenance-counter leak;
  - an explicit window choice by F1, compared against the Kaggle convention.

  Where we're lighter:
  - no operator input;
  - the "agent" is the assistant that helped build the analysis, not a component
    inside it;
  - no deployment or feedback loop.

  One difference in scale: their example precursors appear minutes ahead, on
  fast plant data. Ours appear hours to two days ahead, because this dataset is
  hourly and the warning signs start ~48 h before a failure.

  That's how I'd present it to Oden: the same workflow, compressed into a 
  take-home. Steps 2–4 are done fully and with extra rigour. Steps 1 and 5 are 
  as far as a public dataset allows, and step 5 (playbooks, operator feedback) 
  is the natural next step with real plant input. Notebook 05 section 0
  currently presents the mapping as a straight match. I can update it to include
  these ✅/⚠️ distinctions so it's candid about what's partial. Want me to?

❯ is the ML ebnchmark from prior kaggle submissions? what exactly is it?        

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ No. There are two separate ML pieces, and only one comes from the Kaggle work.

  1. The ML benchmark is our own, not from Kaggle (src/evaluation.py §8). It
  answers one question: "how much better could a flexible model do than our 
  simple rules, scored exactly the same way?"

  - Features (32 per machine-hour, all from past hours only):
    - for each of the 4 signals: raw reading, 6 h mean, 24 h mean, 24 h standard
      deviation (16);
    - for each of the 5 error codes: count in the last 24 h and 48 h (10);
    - hours since each part's last maintenance (4);
    - machine age and model (2).
  - Label: an hour inside the 48 h window before a failure (positive) vs a
    normal-operation hour (negative). Normal hours are subsampled to 40× the
    positives.
  - Training: dev machines only.
  - Three models:
    - gradient boosting, HistGradientBoostingClassifier (200 rounds);
    - the same without the maintenance counters;
    - logistic regression.
  - Turned into alerts like every rule: a model's alert fires when its score
    exceeds P99 of its own normal-operation scores. It's then scored on the
    holdout machines with the same lift, precision, coverage and F1.
  - Results ("any failure", holdout F1): logistic regression 0.63, gradient
    boosting without counters 0.53, gradient boosting with counters 0.32. C18
    scores 0.94.
  - Also: permutation feature importance, measured on holdout machines, as an
    idea source.

  2. The Kaggle model is a replication (src/prior_work.py §9). It rebuilds the
  popular public approach (Microsoft's guide, which most Kaggle notebooks copy):
  - 3-hour rows;
  - 3 h and 24 h signal statistics, 24 h error counts, days since replacement,
    model and age;
  - label "this part fails within the next 24 h";
  - train on earlier months, test on later ones;
  - sklearn's GradientBoostingClassifier, with default settings, as published.

  It reproduced the published score (0.988 vs 0.980). We then changed one thing
  at a time to see what the score depends on. It's scored their way (per 3-hour
  row), not with our lift, precision and coverage. That's why the two aren't
  directly comparable.

  ┌─────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │             │            ML benchmark            │  Kaggle replication  │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Origin      │ ours                               │ Microsoft guide /    │
  │             │                                    │ Kaggle               │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤

⏺ I've documented the ML benchmark in a standalone notebook,
  notebooks/06_ml_benchmark.ipynb. It trains the models live in about 2 minutes,
  and all six notebooks run with no errors.

  What it documents, section by section:
  1. What it is and isn't. Three models used as an idea source and a ceiling,
     not as a detector. It's our own design, separate from the Kaggle
     replication in notebook 04.
  2. Data and window. Identical to the rules: the 48 h before each failure, the
     same definition of normal operation, the same 50 dev and 50 holdout
     machines.
  3. Features. 32 per machine-hour, all from past hours only:
     - telemetry: 16;
     - error counts: 10;
     - maintenance counters: 4;
     - machine age and model: 2.
  4. Labels and training. Window hours vs normal hours, dev machines only. Three
     models: gradient boosting with and without counters, and logistic
     regression.
  5. Scores turned into alerts the same way as every rule (P99 of normal
     operation), then scored with the same metrics and intervals, side by side
     with the kept rules. Also a chart of one machine's score over the year,
     with its failures marked.
  6. What the models rely on (feature importance on holdout machines), why the
     rules win, and the limits of the benchmark.

  The main result:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │                                     │ F1 (holdout) │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Rule C18 matched signature          │ 0.94         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Rule C17 drift + error              │ 0.76         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ ML logistic regression (best model) │ 0.63         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Rule C19 drift on an aging part     │ 0.56         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ ML gradient boosting, no counters   │ 0.52         │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ ML gradient boosting, all features  │ 0.31         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┘

  - The models catch every failure but raise too many false alarms. Their
    precision is 18–46%, against 92% for C18.
  - Without counters, the models rely on the same ingredients as the rules: 6 h
    and 24 h signal averages, and each part's error counts. That confirmed the
    direction of the brainstorm.
  - With counters, gradient boosting leans almost entirely on them and does 
    worst. It's the same trap the past-data check catches in the counter rules.
  - Why the rules win: C18 directly encodes "a part's own signal AND its own
    error code". A generic model must discover that pairing, and the linear
    model can't express it at all.

  Stated limits: there's no tuning, only one threshold, and the models predict
  "any failure" rather than one output per part. It's a sanity ceiling, not the
  best possible ML.

  - notebook 05 (the Oden-workflow mapping);
  - the README's notebook list and findings.

       31 -## Results in one screen                                      
       32 -                                                              
       33 -Window: **"will it fail within the next 48 hours?"** (H = 0, W
          - = 48; the failure hour itself is never                       
       34 -scored). That is the popular Kaggle framing ("within the next 
          -24 hours") extended to cover the whole 48 h                   
       35 -precursor, and it was chosen by F1 (notebook 03, section 6b). 
          -Numbers are from the **50 holdout machines**,                 
       36 -which never influenced any choice. Brackets are 95% cluster-bo
          -otstrap intervals over machines.                              
       37 -                                                              
       38 -**How "good" is scored: F1.** The brief's three numbers (lift,
          - alert precision, event coverage) are each                    
       39 -reported, but each alone can be gamed. Selection and ranking u
          -se **F1 of precision and coverage**: high only                
       40 -when most alarms are real *and* most failures are caught. Befo
          -re that, every detector must pass four gates                  
       41 -on dev machines:                                              
       42 -* a shuffled-timing null test (Bonferroni-corrected);         
       43 -* lift ≥ 2;                                                   
       44 -* coverage ≥ 5%;                                              
       45 -* a causal check: thresholds fitted on Jan–Jun alone must keep
          - ≥ half the Jul–Dec F1.                                       
       46 -                                                              
       47 -Also reported: the precision a random alarm would get (the bar
          - to clear), alerts per machine per month                      
       48 -(alarm load) and lead time.                                   
       49 -                                                              
       50 -**Shortlist for "any component failure"** (350 holdout events;
          - a random alarm would be right 4.1% of the time):             
       51 -                                                              
       52 -| # | Precursor | Rule (simplified) | F1 | Precision | Coverag
          -e | Lift | Alarm load, lead |                                 
       53 -|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|                             
       54 -| 1 | **C18 Matched signature** | a part's **own** signal 6 h 
          -mean beyond its machine's P99 (P1 for rotation) (comp1 volt↑, 
          -comp2 rotate↓, comp3 pressure↑, comp4 vibration↑) **and** that
          - part's **own** error code logged in the last 24 h (error1 / e
          -rror2+3 / error4 / error5) | **0.94** | 92% [90, 95] | 96% [94
          -, 98] | 351× [254, 546] | 1.6 alerts per machine-month, ~24 h 
          -ahead |                                                       
       55 -| 2 | C17 Drift confirmed by an error | any signal's 6 h mean 
          -beyond its machine's median ± 3.72 MAD-σ **and** any error cod
          -e in the last 48 h | 0.76 | 62% [58, 67] | 98% [97, 100] | 37×
          - [32, 44] | 2.3 per machine-month, ~24 h ahead |              
       56 -| 3 | C19 Drift on an aging part | a part's own signal 6 h mea
          -n beyond median ± 3.72 MAD-σ **and** that part older than the 
          -median time since replacement | 0.56 | 41% [36, 45] | 91% [87,
          - 94] | 17× [15, 19] | 4.1 per machine-month, **~43 h ahead** |
       57 -                                                              
       58 -All three beat a shuffled-timing null by z = 44–54 and keep th
          -eir F1 when thresholds are fitted on past data                
       59 -only (Jan–Jun → Jul–Dec: C18 0.93 → 0.91, C17 0.75 → 0.75, C19
          - 0.54 → 0.57). Per component, C18 ranks first                 
       60 -for all four parts: holdout F1 0.94 (comp1), 0.95 (comp2), 0.9
          -5 (comp3), 0.99 (comp4).                                      
       61 -                                                              
       62 -**How to use them together:** C19 is the early watchlist (~2 d
          -ays ahead, 41% precision). C18 is the                         
       63 -"act now" alarm (~1 day ahead, 92% precision). They depend on 
          -different evidence: C18 needs the part's error                
       64 -code, which this data logs exactly 24 h before the failure, wh
          -ile C19 needs only the signal and the part's age.             
       65 -                                                              
       66 -**What we learned**                                           
       67 -                                                              
       68 -1. **The trail is short and sharp.** Each component has one si
          -gnature signal (comp1 volt, comp2 rotate,                     
       69 -   comp3 pressure, comp4 vibration) that shifts by 1.3–2.3 sd 
          -about 48 h before the failure. Failure-linked                 
       70 -   error codes arrive exactly 24 h before. At H = 72 h, W = 16
          -8 h nothing passes for "any failure".                         
       71 -2. **Look-alike excursions cap precision.** About 470 excursio
          -ns per component have the same size and length                
       72 -   but no failure after them. A signal-only rule tops out at ~
          -21–34% precision.                                             
       73 -3. **The part's own error code separates real from look-alike.
          -** 96–100% of real pre-failure excursions                     
       74 -   contain an error, against 14–18% of look-alikes. Requiring 
          -the *matching* code (error1 → comp1,                          
       75 -   error2/3 → comp2, error4 → comp3, error5 → comp4, learned o
          -n dev) separates them further. That is C18,                   
       76 -   an idea taken from prior Kaggle work.                      
       77 -4. **Part age helps before the code arrives.** Real pre-failur
          -e drifts start on parts with a median of 58–73                
       78 -   days since replacement, against ~30 for look-alikes. Combin
          -ing drift with age (C19) raises early-warning                 
       79 -   precision from 28% to ~41% at the same ~2-day lead time.   
       80 -5. **Which approach wins, and by how much:** a **6 h trailing 
          -mean** was the best transform on dev for                      
       81 -   **all 5** event types, with a robust per-machine threshold 
          -(median + z·MAD) in 3 of them. Robust                         
       82 -   thresholds help because the look-alikes fill ~2% of "normal
          -" hours and drag an empirical P99 up.                         
       83 -   Its holdout margin over the brief's own example rule (raw r
          -eading, 3+ consecutive hours) is small and                    
       84 -   inside the intervals. The differences that matter are large
          -r: **CUSUM is clearly worst**, and                            
       85 -   **combining a part's drift with its own error code** lifts 
          -F1 from ~0.44 to ~0.94.                                       
       86 -6. **Simple rules beat our ML benchmark** (notebook 06). Train
          -ed on "pre-failure window vs normal", logistic regression     
       87 -   scores F1 0.63 and gradient boosting without counters 0.53,
          - against 0.94 for C18. With counters, gradient                
       88 -   boosting gets *worse* (0.32). In that labelling "normal" is
          - defined using the events, so a counter becomes               
       89 -   circular.                                                  
       90 -7. **Counters: legitimate as model features, a trap as percent
          -ile rules.** "Hours since maintenance" as a                   
       91 -   stand-alone extreme-percentile rule (C16) scores lift ~1,80
          -0, then collapses once only past data sets its                
       92 -   threshold. The causal gate rejects it. The same counter is 
          -causal and useful inside a time-split model                   
       93 -   (prior-work stress test C vs D), and as a moderate-percenti
          -le condition alongside drift (C19).                           
       94 -                                                              
       95 -**Prior public work.** Most Kaggle notebooks copy Microsoft's 
          -*Predictive Maintenance Modelling Guide*                      
       96 -(3 h and 24 h signal stats, 24 h error counts, days since repl
          -acement, "fails within 24 h" label, time split,               
       97 -one gradient-boosting model, macro F1 ≈ 0.98 on 3-hour rows). 
          -We read its code, rebuilt it, reproduced it                   
       98 -(split 1: 0.988 vs 0.980 published), then changed one thing at
          - a time (`python src/prior_work.py`):                         
       99 -                                                              
      100 -| Variant (macro F1 over none + comp1–4, 3-hour rows, mean of 
          -3 time splits) | Macro F1 | Component F1 |

      109 -At their horizon the error codes carry the score, so counters 
          -don't matter (A ≈ B). At 24–45 h ahead,                       
      110 -before the error code exists, the replacement counter carries 
          -almost everything (C vs D). In the weaker fits one            
      111 -component class collapses to F1 ≈ 0, which looks like fragilit
          -y of a single 5-class boosted model. Our rules                
      112 -score lower on the row-level metric because they raise one ale
          -rt per failure, not a positive on every 3-hour row.           
      113 -                                                              
      114 -We adopted three of its ideas, all tested through the same gat
          -es and scored by F1:                                          
      115 -* its **"will it fail within the next N hours?" framing**, wit
          -h N = 48 instead of 24, because the precursor starts          
      116 -  48 h out. The 24 h version scores lower F1 (notebook 03, sec
          -tion 6b);                                                     
      117 -* **component-specific pairing** (C18);                       
      118 -* **part age combined with drift** (C19).                     
      119 -                                                              
      120 -Two things differ from it. The failure hour itself is never sc
          -ored, because it contains the repair record.                  
      121 -And metrics are counted per alert and per failure, not per 3-h
          -our row, where 98% of rows are trivially "none"               
      122 -and each failure counts eight times. Details are in notebook 0
          -4, section 6.                                                 
      123 -                                                              
      124 -**Confidence:** with 699 evaluable events the intervals are ti
          -ght (±3–5 points on precision for "any";                      
      125 -widest for comp3 with 62 holdout events). The bigger risk is e
          -xternal validity. The data is synthetic                       
      126 -(exact 48 h shifts, errors at exactly 24 h, 06:00 stamps), so 
          -treat the absolute numbers as optimistic.                     
      127 -Error-based rules (C18, C17) depend on the error code that thi
          -s data logs exactly 24 h before a failure.                    
      128 -The chosen window contains it comfortably, but with a real, no
          -n-batched error log that timing should be                     
      129 -re-checked.                                                   
      130 -The method and the ranking of approaches should transfer bette
          -r than the numbers.
