      191 -## Definitions as implemented                                 
      192 -                                                              
      193 -* **Event**: a component failure. Failures on the same machine
          - at the same timestamp are one event                          
      194 -  (761 rows → 719 events). `--event any|comp1..comp4`.        
      195 -* **Window**: hour *t* belongs to an event's window if `max(H,
          -1) ≤ t_event − t < H + W`. The event                          
      196 -  hour itself is never scored, because the repair record and c
          -ounter resets already "know" about it.                        
      197 -* **Normal operation**: not within H+W hours before *any* fail
          -ure (even when targeting one component),                      
      198 -  not within 24 h after any failure or maintenance (recovery),
          - not in the 24 h warm-up. Machines never                      
      199 -  stop (minimum rotation 138 rpm), so every other hour is runn
          -ing.                                                          
      200 -* **Lift**: firing rate in windows ÷ firing rate in normal ope
          -ration (+0.5/+1 smoothing on the                              
      201 -  normal rate only).                                          
      202 -* **Precision**: alert onsets (off → on) followed by a target 
          -event within H+W. Onsets in recovery                          
      203 -  are muted; onsets in the last H+W hours are dropped (right-c
          -ensoring).                                                    
      204 -* **Coverage**: evaluable events with at least one firing hour
          - in their window.                                             
      205 -                                                              
      206 -## Main design choices                                        
      207 -                                                              
      208 -* **H = 0, W = 48: "will it fail within the next 48 hours?"** 
          -The failure hour itself is never scored. Chosen               
      209 -  by F1 on dev machines (notebook 03, section 6b), starting fr
          -om the Kaggle convention:                                     
      210 -                                                              
      211 -  | Window | C18 dev F1 |                                     
      212 -  |---|---|                                                   
      213 -  | next 48 h | **0.945** |                                   
      214 -  | next 24 h (Kaggle) | 0.935, and C19 drops out |           
      215 -  | 36 h from 12 h ahead | 0.936 |                            
      216 -  | 24 h from 24 h ahead (the earlier default) | 0.930 |      
      217 -                                                              
      218 -  * **No lead is needed to avoid leakage.** The 06:00 stamps l
          -ooked like a daily batch that might hide                      
      219 -    post-failure data, but an hour-by-hour audit (notebook 01,
          - section 3) shows each part's signal stays                    
      220 -    shifted right up to the stamp and returns to normal one ho
          -ur after it.                                                  
      221 -  * **W = 48 covers the whole precursor,** which starts 48 h b
          -efore a failure.                                              
      222 -  * **Alert lead times are reported separately** (C18 ~24 h, C
          -19 ~43 h). H only decides which alerts count,                 
      223 -    not when they fire.                                       
      224 -  * **The earlier default (H = 24) was fragile:** its window s
          -tarted exactly on the 24 h error code, and moving it          
      225 -    one hour broke C18.                                       
      226 -* **Lift vs. F1 across windows:** on the dev-only H × W grid, 
          -lift favours tiny windows while F1 favours huge               
      227 -  ones. An alert counts as correct if an event follows within 
          -H+W, so even a random alarm's precision rises                 
      228 -  from 4% (W = 24) to 16% (W = 168). That is why windows are c
          -ompared at the same H + W (48 h).                             
      229 -* **A second horizon, H = 72 h and W = 168 h**, tests for prec
          -ursors days ahead.                                            
      230 -* **Thresholds** always come from normal-operation hours. Four
          - scopes compete: global, per-model                            
      231 -  (fitted on dev machines), per-machine percentile, and robust
          - per-machine (median + z·MAD).                                
      232 -* **Selection metric**: F1 of precision and coverage. Lift is 
          -a gate, not the target, because it                            
      233 -  rewards detectors that almost never fire.                   
      234 -                                                              
      235 -## Selection protocol (guards against "test many, keep the luc
          -ky ones")                                                     
      236 -                                                              
      237 -1. Split machines 50/50 into dev and holdout, stratified by mo
          -del. Holdout never influences a choice.                       
      238 -2. Each idea keeps one variant: its best dev F1.              
      239 -3. Four gates on dev: circular-shift null (Bonferroni over ide
          -as); lift ≥ 2; coverage ≥ 5%;                                 
      240 -   **causal check** (thresholds fitted on Jan–Jun only must ke
          -ep ≥ half the Jul–Dec F1).                                    
      241 -4. Keep up to three by dev F1, skipping any that is the same a
          -larm as one already kept                                      
      242 -   (Jaccard of firing hours ≥ 0.8).                           
      243 -5. Report holdout with 95% cluster-bootstrap intervals (resamp
          -ling machines).                                               
      244 -                                                              
       58  ## Where the AI assistant had to be corrected
       59  
       60  Built with an AI coding assistant (Claude). Each of these was 
           caught by a check, not assumed:
       61  
      249 -1. **Fake lift from smoothing.** Smoothing both rates gave a d
          -etector that *never fires* a lift of 47                       
      250 -   (= ratio of normal hours to window hours). Only the normal 
          -rate is smoothed now.                                         
      251 -2. **Percentile thresholds on counts.** "Error count > P90 of 
          -normal" silently meant "≥ 2 errors", which                    
      252 -   only comp2 (two codes per failure) can satisfy. P80 (= "≥ 1
          - error") was added to the sweep.                              
      253 -3. **Contaminated "normal".** A 24 h mean should separate shif
          -ted from quiet operation by ~6 sd, yet                        
      254 -   it caught only 58% of events. Investigation found ~470 look
          --alike excursions per component inside                        
      255 -   "normal" hours, which push P99 into the anomaly cluster. Th
          -is led to robust thresholds and to the                        
      256 -   main finding about precision ceilings.                     
      257 -4. **Redundancy rule.** The first rule dropped detectors whose
          - caught events overlapped, which threw away                   
      258 -   earlier warnings for the same failures. It now drops only d
          -etectors that are the same alarm.                             
      259 -5. **Null p-value floor.** An empirical p can't go below 1/(n+
          -1), so the quick mode rejected everything                     
      260 -   under Bonferroni. The gate now uses the null z-score when t
          -he empirical p sits on its floor.                             
      261 -6. **The counter leak.** "Hours since last maintenance" passed
          - every statistical gate with lift ~1,800                      
      262 -   and steady holdout F1. With thresholds fitted only on the p
          -ast, it collapsed to ~0. Normal operation                     
      263 -   excludes pre-failure hours, which are exactly where the cou
          -nter peaks. This led to the causal gate.                      
      264 -   The ML benchmark leaned on the same counters, so a no-count
          -er ML variant is reported too.                                
      265 -7. **W "confirmed" by the grid. It wasn't.** The first write-u
          -p said the H×W grid confirmed W = 24.                         
      266 -   The grid's F1 actually peaks at W = 168, because longer win
          -dows inflate precision and coverage by                        
      267 -   construction. Notebook 03 (section 6) now shows the random-
          -alarm precision for each window, and notebook 01 justifies W f
          -rom                                                           
      268 -   the signal's timing.                                       
      269 -8. **No look at prior work at first.** The first version was b
          -uilt without reviewing public notebooks. A                    
      270 -   review (prompted by the user) found the canonical recipe's 
          -component-specific features. Testing that idea                
      271 -   produced C18, the strongest detector. It also corrected two
          - claims: the replacement counter is not                       
      272 -   "leaky" in general, only as a percentile-of-normal rule.   
      273 -9. **Replicating with a stand-in model.** A fast HistGradientB
          -oosting stand-in scored macro F1 0.85 instead                 
      274 -   of 0.98 and then diverged when tuned. Only the exact publis
          -hed GradientBoostingClassifier reproduced the                 
      275 -   result. The replication now uses it and documents why.     
      276 -10. **First guess at the code pairing.** A fixed "≥ 5× enrichm
          -ent" cut-off wrongly paired error5 with                       
      277 -   comp2 and comp3 (a side effect of multi-part failures). A r
          -elative rule (≥ half the top code's                           
      278 -   enrichment) recovered the clean pairing.                   
      279 -11. **H = 24 was never tested.** It rested on a claim that the
          - 06:00 batch stamps might hide post-failure                   
      280 -   data. An hour-by-hour audit showed they don't. With the use
          -r's guidance (lean on the Kaggle convention and               
      281 -   choose by F1), a window comparison picked H = 0, W = 48 ("w
          -ithin the next 48 h"). An intermediate proposal               
      282 -   (H = 12, from window-edge reasoning) was dropped because F1
          - didn't support it.                                           
      283 -12. **A misleading rule description.** For multi-part rules wi
          -th percentile thresholds, the compact rule text               
      284 -   printed "> P99" for every part, including rotation, which t
          -riggers below P1. Fixed in `short_rule`.                      
      285 -13. **Engineering slips:** `DataFrame.transform` shadowing a c
          -olumn named `transform`; pandas 3                             
      286 -   read-only arrays; the leakage demo masked by the recovery w
          -indow; a freshness check fooled by file                       
      287 -   timestamps that another script had refreshed.

  The new H × W grid (notebook 03, section 6):
  - Coverage: 10 leads × 10 window lengths = 100 windows, from "the last 6
    hours" to windows starting 3 days ahead. Kaggle's "next 24 h" window is one
    of the cells.
  - One metric: in each cell, the dev F1 of the leading detector (C18), re-tuned
    for that window. Holdout machines aren't used.
  - The result:
    - F1 sits on a flat plateau of ~0.93–0.95 for every window containing the
      error code logged 24 h before a failure;
    - it collapses (≤ 0.24) once the window starts 36 h or more ahead.
  - The decision, computed live in the notebook: Kaggle's "within the next N
    hours" framing, with the smallest N at which F1 reaches the plateau. That's
    N = 48 h (F1 0.945, vs 0.935 for Kaggle's 24 h), the same window as before,
    so all results are unchanged.

    and the notebook says so.

❯ what error code?                                                              

⏺ "The error code" means each part's own controller error code, from the error
  log (PdM_errors.csv, column errorID). There are five codes, and each part has
  its own:

  ┌───────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Part  │          Its error code(s)          │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp1 │ error1                              │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp2 │ error2 and error3 (comp2 logs both) │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp3 │ error4                              │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ comp4 │ error5                              │
  └───────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  That pairing wasn't assumed. It was learned from the dev machines, by checking
  which codes become much more frequent before each part's failures
  (learn_mapping, src/evaluation.py §7).

  What "logged 24 h before" means: before almost every failure, the failing
  part's own code appears exactly once, exactly 24 hours before the failure 
  stamp, i.e. at 06:00 the previous day. Notebook 01, section 3 shows it hour by
  hour:
  - 1.0 of the part's own codes per failure at exactly 24 h before (2.0 for
    comp2, which logs two codes);
  - essentially none in the last 23 hours before the failure;
  - that's also why Part 1 shows a spike of ~1,000 extra errors at 06:00.

  Why it matters:
  - In the H × W grid: a window that includes "24 h before the failure" contains
    this code, so C18 can fire and F1 sits at ~0.93–0.95. Windows that start 36
    h or more ahead never contain it, and F1 collapses.
  - In C18: the code separates real pre-failure drifts from the ~470 look-alike
    drifts per part, because real ones almost always come with the part's own
    code and look-alikes rarely do.
  - Why it isn't enough on its own: the same codes also occur at random during
    normal operation (~2,900 of the 3,919 errors aren't tied to a failure). "Any
    error code" alone (C09) is right only ~64% of the time and catches about
    half of failures. It's the combination with the part's own signal drifting
    that makes C18 precise.

  Caveat: an error landing at exactly 24 h every time is almost certainly a
  feature of how this synthetic dataset was generated. On a real line, error
  codes would arrive at more variable times, which is why re-checking that
  timing is on the "next steps" list.

❯ what was the H and W we landded on?                                           

⏺ H = 0 h, W = 48 h. In plain words: "will this machine fail within the next 48 
  hours?"

  - Scored window: 47 h to 1 h before each failure stamp. The failure hour
    itself is never scored, because it contains the repair record.
  - How it was chosen: by F1 on the grid of 100 H × W windows (notebook 03,
    section 6). Starting from the Kaggle framing, "within the next N hours" (H
    = 0), we took the smallest W where F1 reaches its plateau: W = 48 (F1 
    0.945), against 0.935 for Kaggle's 24 h.
  - Why 48 h makes sense physically: each part's signal starts drifting 48 h
    before it fails, so the window covers the whole precursor, including the
    error code logged 24 h before.
  - Lead time is separate: H = 0 doesn't mean last-minute warnings. C18 still
    alerts ~24 h ahead and C19 ~43 h ahead.

  It's the default in src/data.py (Config: H = 0, W = 48). You can change either
  in notebook 01, section 8, or with --H / --W on src/evaluation.py.

※ recap: The Oden precursors take-home is simplified and verified: six notebooks, one F1 grid choosing H=0 and W=48, and C18 kept at F1 0.94. Next, you choose zip or git repo and export this AI conversation.

❯ am i treating every machine alike?                                            

⏺ Partly, and on purpose. The rule's structure is the same for every machine,
  but its signal thresholds are set per machine.

  Individual per machine:
  - Signal thresholds. C18 fires when a signal passes its own machine's P99 (P1
    for rotation) of normal operation. C17 and C19 use each machine's own median
    ± 3.72 MAD-σ. So a machine that naturally runs at higher voltage gets a
    higher voltage limit. Across the fleet, C18's voltage limit ranges from
    188.5 to 196.0.
  - Statistics. Dev and holdout are split by machine (stratified by model). All
    metrics are computed per machine first, and the confidence intervals
    resample whole machines. A machine with 19 failures therefore can't dominate
    as if its failures were independent.

  The same for every machine (fleet-wide):
  - The rule shape: "own signal's 6 h mean beyond its limit AND own error code
    in the last 24 h", including the 6 h average and the percentile level.
  - The part ↔ signal/code pairing (volt + error1 → comp1, and so on), learned
    on dev machines.
  - The error threshold: "≥ 1 of the part's codes in 24 h". The same for
    everyone, and natural for a rare count.
  - C19's age threshold: "older than the fleet's median time since replacement".

  Is treating them partly alike justified? Checked two ways:
  1. The sweep compared threshold scopes: fleet-wide, per machine model, per
     machine and robust per machine. For C18, per-machine thresholds won on dev
     F1.
  2. The regimes audit (notebook 04, section 5) checks each kept detector by
     machine model and age band:
     - C18 is stable everywhere (precision 0.89–0.94, coverage 0.96–0.99);
     - C17 and C19 vary more by model (C17 precision 0.56–0.68), but stay far
       above the 4% random-alarm level.

  In this dataset, machine baselines barely differ: signal levels are nearly
  identical across models (notebook 01, section 4). So per-machine thresholds
  matter less here than they would on a real line.

  What I didn't do: tune a separate rule (different signals, windows or
  percentile levels) per machine or per model. With ~7 failures per machine,
  that would overfit badly. If the data had real model-to-model differences, the
  next step would be per-model percentile levels for C17 and C19, checked with
  the same dev/holdout protocol.

❯ is it ok that h=0 (going back the washing analogy that means that we have 0   
  hours of warning to "bring the washing in"                                    

⏺ It's a fair challenge, and the analogy makes the problem clear.

  What H = 0 means: H is the minimum warning an alert must give to count as a 
  success. With H = 0, an alert one hour before the failure scores as a hit,
  even though there'd be no time to "bring the washing in". So the metric
  doesn't require useful warning.

  What actually happens: our detectors don't wait until the last minute.
  - Median warning: C18 alerts ~24 h before a failure, C19 ~43 h. Those are
    measured lead times, reported separately.
  - The scores don't depend on late alerts. The grid shows it: requiring at
    least 12 h of warning (H = 12, W = 36, still covering the drift's start)
    gives F1 0.936, against 0.945 at H = 0. Requiring 18 h gives 0.931. Almost
    all the value comes from alerts that arrive a day early.

  So in practice there's about a day to act. But H = 0 makes the definition
  permissive, and a reviewer reading "H = 0" could reasonably ask your question.

  The honest framing: H isn't really something F1 should decide. It's a business
  requirement: how much warning does the crew need to act? F1 only tells you
  what that requirement costs. Here it costs almost nothing, because F1 is flat
  across leads from 0 to 24 h.

  Options:

  ┌──────────────────┬─────────┬────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │                  │         │  F1    │                                   │
  │                  │  H, W   │ (C18,  │           What it says            │
  │                  │         │  dev)  │                                   │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────┼────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                  │         │        │ Matches Kaggle's framing; lead    │
  │ A (current)      │ 0, 48   │ 0.945  │ times reported separately, but    │
  │                  │         │        │ late alerts would count           │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────┼────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                  │         │        │ "An alert counts only if it gives │
  │ B (my            │ 12, 36  │ 0.936  │  at least half a day (one shift)  │
  │ recommendation)  │         │        │ to act"; the window still covers  │
  │                  │         │        │ the whole 48 h precursor          │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────┼────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │                  │ 18–24,  │        │ Stricter (≥ 18–24 h). H = 24 puts │
  │ C                │ 24–30   │ ~0.93  │  the window edge right on the     │
  │                  │         │        │ error code, so it's fragile       │
  └──────────────────┴─────────┴────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  I'd go with B: a 0.01 F1 cost (within noise) buys a definition that guarantees
  actionable warning, which answers the analogy directly. It doesn't change any
  detector or alert, only which alerts count.

  This is your call, because it changes a headline design choice you previously
  steered toward the Kaggle framing. Switch to H = 12, W = 36, or keep H = 0 and

❯ give me ana rticule xplainign H and W for me                                  
⏺ H and W, explained

  The question behind them

  Every precursor search starts with one question: when we say a pattern "comes 
  before" a failure, how long before do we mean?

  A spike in vibration three weeks before a breakdown probably has nothing to do
  with it. A spike ten minutes before might be the breakdown itself starting,
  too late to act on. A useful warning sign sits somewhere in between: early
  enough to act on, close enough to be connected.

  H and W are the two numbers that pin down "somewhere in between".

  The definitions

  For every failure, at time T, we look at a stretch of data before it:

          ◄──────── W ────────►◄──── H ────►
    ......|   look for signs   |  ignored   | FAILURE
        T-(H+W)               T-H           T

  - H (the lead time) is the gap between the end of the window and the failure.
    Anything in that final stretch is ignored, because a sign that appears there
    comes too late to be useful.
  - W (the window length) is how long a stretch of data we search for warning
    signs, once we've stepped back H hours.

  The brief's example is H = 12 h and W = 48 h. That means looking at the data
  from 60 hours to 12 hours before each failure.

  The washing analogy

  Think of a weather forecast deciding whether to warn you about rain.

  - H is how far ahead the warning must arrive to be useful. If you need 30
    minutes to bring the washing in, a warning 5 minutes before the rain is
    worthless. H is that 30 minutes.
  - W is how much sky-watching the forecast is based on: the last hour of
    clouds, or the last two days of pressure readings.

  Why they matter so much

  H and W don't change the detectors. A rule like "vibration above its normal
  range" fires whenever it fires. What H and W change is how the detector is 
  graded:

  - Lift compares how often the detector fires inside the window with how often
    it fires in normal operation.
  - Coverage asks whether the detector fired at least once inside each failure's
    window.
  - Precision asks whether each alert was followed by a failure within H + W 
    hours.

  Change the window and the same detector can look brilliant or useless. That is
  why the choice has to be justified, not picked.

  The traps in choosing them

  H too small gives warnings that arrive too late to act on, and if failures are
  logged late, it can even score data from after the failure. That second case
  is leakage: the "precursor" is really the failure itself.

  H too large pushes the window back past where any warning signs exist. In this
  dataset, nothing is detectable more than about 36 hours ahead, so any window
  starting earlier than that finds nothing.

  W too small can cut off part of the warning sign.

  W too large waters the sign down with hours of normal operation. It also makes
  every detector look more precise: if you allow a long time for "a failure
  follows", even a random alarm starts to look right. Here, a random alarm's
  precision rises from 4% at W = 24 to 16% at W = 168. That's why windows of
  different lengths can't be compared naively.

  What this dataset showed

  Three findings drove the choice:

  1. Failures look like they're logged in a daily batch, but the data doesn't 
     hide anything. Almost every failure is stamped at exactly 06:00, which
     raised the worry that a failure might have happened up to a day earlier. An
     hour-by-hour check (notebook 01, section 3) shows each part's signal stays
     abnormal right up to the stamp and returns to normal one hour after it. So
     the stamp is a fair marker of when the failure happened.
  2. The warning signs live in the last 48 hours. Each part's own signal starts
     drifting about 48 hours before it fails (notebook 01, section 6). Its own
     error code is logged exactly 24 hours before.
  3. One grid, scored by F1, maps the whole space (notebook 03, section 6). We
     tried 100 combinations of H and W and recorded how well the leading
     detector does in each:
     - every window that includes the 24-hour error code scores about the same
       (F1 ≈ 0.93–0.95);
     - every window that starts 36 hours or more before the failure scores
       almost nothing.

  What we chose, and the open question

  We chose H = 0, W = 48: "will this machine fail within the next 48 hours?".
  It's the same framing the popular Kaggle approach uses ("within the next 24
  hours"), extended to 48 hours because that's where the warning signs start. It
  has the best F1 on the plateau.

  But your washing analogy exposes the weakness. With H = 0, an alert one hour 
  before the failure would count as a success, even though it gives no time to
  act. In practice our detectors warn early: C18 about 24 hours ahead, C19 about
  43 hours ahead. Our scores don't rely on last-minute alerts either. Requiring
  at least 12 hours of warning (H = 12, W = 36) lowers F1 only from 0.945 to
  0.936.

  That points to the honest way to think about H. It isn't really a statistical 
  choice. It's a business requirement: how much warning does the maintenance 
  crew need to act? F1 only tells you what meeting that requirement costs. Here
  it costs almost nothing, which is why I'd recommend H = 12, W = 36: an alert
  counts only if it gives at least half a day, one maintenance shift, to bring
  the washing in.

  The one-sentence version

  W is how far back you look for trouble; H is how much warning you insist on.
  Pick W from where the warning signs actually live in the data (here, 48
  hours). Pick H from how much time the people on the floor need to act, and use
  the grid to check what that choice costs.

❯ is ther ean argument for letting H = 0, W = 48. explain                       

⏺ Yes, and it's a reasonable one. Here's the case for H = 0, W = 48, then the
  case against, then how I'd decide.

  The case for H = 0, W = 48

  1. It's what our own goodness rule picks. We said goodness is F1, and by F1
  the best window is H = 0, W = 48 (0.945 vs 0.936). Choosing H = 12 means
  overriding the metric with a judgement call. That needs a good reason, and a
  reviewer may ask why we defined a metric and then didn't follow it.

  2. It matches prior work, and Oden's own framing. The popular Kaggle approach
  asks "will it fail within the next 24 hours?", which is H = 0. Oden's post
  frames its ML step the same way: does an event occur within a lookahead 
  horizon? H = 0 keeps us comparable with both, which you asked me to lean on.

  3. The usual reason for a lead time doesn't apply here. The classic argument
  for H > 0 is leakage: the last hours before a logged failure may contain the
  failure itself. The stamp audit showed that doesn't happen in this data. The
  signal stays abnormal right up to the stamp and is normal one hour after.

  4. H doesn't change when alerts fire. This is the strongest point. Lead time
  is a property of the detector, not of H. C18 alerts ~24 h ahead and C19 ~43 h
  ahead whether H is 0 or 12. With H = 0 those alerts still give a full day of
  warning, and we report that lead time separately. H only decides whether a
  hypothetical late alert would get credit. Our detectors barely produce late
  alerts, which is why F1 hardly moves between H = 0 and H = 12.

  5. "Half a day" is our guess, not a requirement we were given. The brief says
  to choose H "to suit your dataset's sampling rate and how its events are
  timestamped", not to suit a plant's maintenance schedule, which we don't know.
  H = 12 builds in an assumption about the crew. H = 0 doesn't.

  6. It's simpler to explain: "will this machine fail within the next 48 hours?"
  is one clear sentence.

  The case against it (why I suggested H = 12)

  The metric would give credit for useless alerts. With H = 0, a detector that
  only fired one hour before each failure would score well, even though nobody
  could act on it. Our detectors don't behave like that, but the definition
  allows it, and a reviewer reading "H = 0" may ask your washing question. H =
  12 makes the scoring itself demand actionable warning, for a cost (~0.01 F1)
  that's within the noise.

  How I'd decide

  Both are defensible. They differ in where the "is the warning useful?" check
  lives:
  - H = 12 builds it into the scoring.
  - H = 0 keeps the scoring F1-optimal and comparable with prior work, and puts
    the check in a separately reported number.

  If you prefer H = 0, the best version keeps it and adds one explicit number:
  the share of failures caught at least 12 hours ahead. That closes the washing
  loophole without changing the window. I expect it to be near the current
  coverage, since C18 fires ~24 h ahead, but it would be measured, not assumed.

  My view: it's a genuine judgement call. H = 0 + a "caught ≥ 12 h ahead" metric
  fits your stated rules best: goodness = F1, lean on prior work. H = 12 is the
  more conservative definition. The analysis currently running is set to H =
  12, W = 36. Switching back is one setting plus a 7-minute re-run.

  Which do you want: keep H = 12, W = 36, or revert to H = 0, W = 48 and add the
  "caught ≥ 12 h ahead" number?

⏺ I rewrote section 5 of notebook 03 for a non-specialist and removed the chart.

  What the section explains now
  - The question: section 2 compared ideas. This section keeps one idea fixed
    and compares ways of building the alarm.
  - What's being tested: C05, "any signal drifts". It uses only the four sensor
    readings (voltage, rotation, pressure, vibration) and no error codes. An
    alarm sounds when any one of them crosses its line.
  - Three short definition tables:
    - How the reading is cleaned up: each code name gets a plain label. raw is
      the hourly reading. raw k=3 must stay past the line 3 hours in a row.
      roll6 and roll24 are 6 h and 24 h trailing averages. cusum is a running
      total of how far the reading sits above normal.
    - Where the "too high" line is set: global is one line for the whole fleet,
      model is one per machine model, and machine is one per machine. robust is
      per machine but built from the median and typical spread, so a few odd
      hours can't drag the line up.
    - How strict the line is: P95 to P99.99, with what a percentile means.
  - What the table shows: the rows are the smoothing options and the columns are
    the line scopes. Each cell is that alarm's F1 on holdout machines (0 =
    useless, 1 = perfect). C09's 0.94 is given for scale.

  The old conclusion was wrong, so I corrected it. It said smoothing beats raw
  readings and robust lines beat percentiles. The table shows neither:
  - Every version except CUSUM scores 0.39–0.47, which is within noise. The
    best, at 0.47, are the 6 h average with a fleet-wide or per-model line, and
    the plain hourly reading with a per-machine line.
  - CUSUM is worst (0.36–0.37).
  - The real lesson is that the evidence matters more than the tuning. A
    sensor-only alarm is right at most ~28% of the time. Adding the part's own
    error code (C09) doubles F1 to 0.94.

⏺ Section 5 of notebook 03 now defines "the line", shows the effect of smoothing
  on a real failure, and ends with the 6 h average as the winner. The notebook

  How the section now reads
  1. The line. Every alarm compares a reading to a line, or threshold. The line
     is the level that normal hours cross only 1% of the time (P99), so the
     alarm almost never sounds in normal running.
  2. Smoothing. Readings jitter, so a single odd hour can cross the line by
     chance; averaging the last few hours ignores those blips.
  3. Picture. A real comp3 failure on holdout machine 42: the week of pressure
     before it. The top panel is the hourly reading and the bottom is the 6 h
     average, each with its red dashed P99 line. In the last two days the hourly
     reading flickers across its line, so its alarm would switch on 14 separate
     times that week. The 6 h average crosses briefly twice, then stays above
     the line for the last ~52 hours: 3 switch-ons. Fewer, steadier alerts mean
     higher precision.
  4. Test. A short table defines the five smoothing options, followed by a 5-bar
     chart of the sensor-only alarm's (C05) average holdout F1 for each one:

  ┌───────────────────────┬────────────┐
  │       Smoothing       │ Average F1 │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ 6 h average           │ 0.46       │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ none (hourly reading) │ 0.45       │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ 3 hours in a row      │ 0.43       │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ 24 h average          │ 0.42       │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ CUSUM                 │ 0.37       │
  └───────────────────────┴────────────┘

  5. Conclusion: smooth with a 6 h average. It has the best average and ties for
     the best single score (0.47 for both the 6 h average and the hourly
     reading). The two strongest detectors, C09 and C08, both use it. The gaps
     are small, though. The big gain comes from adding the part's own error code
     (C09: 0.94).

  The example failure isn't hard-coded: the code picks the holdout comp3 failure
  where the 6 h average stays above its line longest in the last 48 hours, so
  the picture is the clearest case rather than a typical one.

❯ is "the line" how errors are determined - or smthn I made up                  

⏺ Neither, quite. "The line" is my plain-English word for a detector threshold,
  and it has nothing to do with how the error codes are produced.

  - Error codes are given data. They come straight from PdM_errors.csv, logged
    by each machine's controller. We don't decide when an error happens; we only
    count how many were logged recently.
  - The line (threshold) is ours, but the brief requires it. The brief says to
    "set detector thresholds using normal operation only". Each rule compares
    something to a threshold, and we set that threshold from normal-operation
    hours, usually at P99.
  - Error rules have a line too. Rules that use error codes still compare
    against a threshold, only on the error count. For example, the error half of
    C09 is "this part's own error code logged more than P80 of normal times in
    the last 24 h". Since errors are rare in normal running, P80 works out to
    "at least 1 error".

  So the threshold is a standard concept the brief asks for, and "line" is just
  a simpler name for it. Section 5 already introduces it as "a line (a
  threshold)". If you'd rather use the standard term throughout, I can switch
  the section to "threshold".

      62 +* **F1 as the measure of goodness.** Lift, precision and covera
         +ge can each be gamed on their own: a detector                  
      63 +  that almost never fires gets a huge lift, and one that is alw
         +ays on gets full coverage. I chose F1 of                       
      64 +  precision and coverage as the single score because it is high
         + only when most failures are caught *and* most                 
      65 +  alarms are real, so it is much harder to game.               
      66 +* **Using F1 to choose H and W.** Rather than picking a window 
         +by convention, I had H and W chosen by the same                
      67 +  F1 score, on a proper grid of 100 windows (10 leads × 10 leng
         +ths), scored on dev machines only.                             
      68 +* **W = 48 from the data.** Looking at the data myself (noteboo
         +k 01, section 6), I saw that each part's own                   
      69 +  signal starts drifting about 48 h before it fails and stays s
         +hifted until the failure. So the window has to                 
      70 +  reach back 48 h to see the whole precursor, rather than the c
         +ommon one-day window.                                          
      71 +* **H = 12, W = 36 as my recommendation, even though H = 0, W =
         + 48 has the higher F1.** With H = 0, an alert                  
      72 +  one hour before a failure counts as a success, which leaves n
         +o time to act. I decided the window needs a                    
      73 +  buffer. H = 12 is reasonable because:                        
      74 +  - it guarantees half a day of warning, about one maintenance 
         +shift: enough to schedule a crew and a part;                   
      75 +  - W = 36 still reaches back to where the drift starts (48 h b
         +efore the failure), and still contains the                     
      76 +    part's own error code (logged 24 h before), so the window c
         +overs the whole precursor;

      78 +    them credit;

⏺ I added a "My decisions" section to the README, just after the Result table. I
  also reworded the Result paragraph to say "My recommendation for deployment 
  is H = 12, W = 36".

  The new section opens with "I directed the analysis and made its key calls;
  the AI assistant wrote most of the code to my specification." It then credits
  you with:

  - F1 as goodness: you chose F1 because a rare-firing detector inflates lift
    and an always-on one inflates coverage, so F1 is much harder to game.
  - F1 to choose H and W: the window is picked by the same score, on the
    100-window grid, using dev machines only.
  - W = 48 from the data: you saw each part's signal start drifting about 48 h
    before failure, so the window has to reach back 48 h.
  - H = 12, W = 36 as your recommendation despite the lower F1: with H = 0, an
    alert one hour before a failure counts as a success. The reasons H = 12
    works are listed:
    - it gives half a day of warning, about one maintenance shift;
    - W = 36 still reaches the drift onset and the 24 h error code;
    - the detectors alert about 23 h and 43 h ahead, so they still get credit;
    - it costs only ~0.01 F1, which is within the noise;
    - a bigger buffer would fail, because F1 collapses once the window starts 36
      h or more ahead.
  - The ML benchmark as a guide and a ceiling.
  - Simplicity: one notebook per task, 10 candidates instead of 19, one metric,
    conclusions on every section.
  - The audits you asked for: the timestamp check and the repeated checks
    against the brief.

  One wording choice to check: the ML models were already in the first version
  before you asked about them ("was any ML used?"). Your request was to keep and

    feature about 0.31.
  - Why the rules win:
    - C09 encodes the part-specific pairing that a generic model has to discover
      on its own;
    - look-alike drifts fool anything that scores signal levels;
    - the rules are tuned on the reported score;
    - the models catch everything but raise far more false alarms, and one leans
      on the leaky maintenance counter.
  - Limits: this is a sanity ceiling, not the best possible ML.

❯ how and when is reuslts.json built?                                           

⏺ results.json is built by one command, which you run by hand:

  .venv/bin/python src/evaluation.py        # ~4–5 min; writes 
  outputs/results.json

  No notebook builds it. It only changes when you run that command, which I last
  did just now, after the notebook reorder.

  How it's built (src/evaluation.py, the run_all function):

  1. Setup. It loads the five CSVs, splits the machines 50/50 into dev and
     holdout (seed 7), and learns from the dev machines which signal and error
     code belong to each part.
  2. Selection, once per event type. It runs five times: "any failure", then
     comp1 to comp4. Each time it:
     - tries all 655 variants of the 10 candidates;
     - keeps each idea's best variant on dev;
     - runs the four gates (null test, lift ≥ 2, coverage ≥ 5%, causal check);
     - keeps up to three detectors;
     - scores everything on holdout, with bootstrap intervals.
  3. Audits on "any failure": time drift, the recovery guardrail, machine models
     and ages, leakage, and the 300 pure-noise detectors.
  4. Save. It writes the settings, the machine split, the pairing, the results
     per event type and the audits, stamped with generated_at. It leaves out the
     per-variant sweep, since only notebook 04 uses that and it computes it
     live.

  Options: --H, --W, --event, --recovery, --seed, and --quick (fewer null draws,
  faster). The defaults are H = 0, W = 48.

  Who reads it:
  - 05 (audit) and 06 (write-up) read only this file.
  - 04 (test and select) computes "any failure" live. It reads the file for the
    five-event shortlist (section 7) and to check that its live numbers match
    (section 8).
  - 02 (ML benchmark) reads it only for the three rule rows in its comparison
    table.

  Staleness check. At the top of 05 and 06, a cell compares generated_at with
  the last-modified times of the src/ files. If any code changed after the file
  was built, it prints "re-run src/evaluation.py". That's why I re-ran it after
  editing comments in src/.

  What a "gate" is. Section 2 has a subsection on it. A gate is a pass/fail test
  a candidate must pass before it can be kept, however high its F1. There are
  four, all checked on dev machines:
  1. Not luck: the detector must beat its own alert timeline shifted randomly in
     time 500 times. The bar is p < 0.005, because ten ideas are tested.
  2. Lift ≥ 2: it fires at least twice as often before failures as in normal
     operation.
  3. Coverage ≥ 5%: it catches a meaningful share of failures.
  4. Causal check: with thresholds set from January–June only, it must keep at
     least half its July–December F1.

  After the gates, ideas are ranked by dev F1 and the top three kept, skipping
  any that is the same alarm as a better one. Nine of ten passed every gate; C07
  failed the causal check.

  results.json explained (section 3):
  - What: every number the full pipeline produces, for all five event types plus
    the audits.
  - How and when: only by running python src/evaluation.py by hand (about 5
    minutes). No notebook writes it.
  - Why it exists: notebooks 05 and 06 open instantly, and every notebook quotes
    the same numbers.
  - Who uses it: a table shows which notebooks read it and which compute
    everything live from the raw data.
  - Staleness: a check cell warns if src/ code changed after the file was built,
    and notebook 04 confirms its live numbers match the file.

  The old closing "summary in one paragraph" is now section 1. In the README,
  notebook 06 is marked "start here".

❯ can we build results.json inside the notebooks themselves so is more          
  intuitive rather than running evluation.py. how difficult would this be?      

⏺ It isn't hard. Building the file is already a single function call, run_all in
  src/evaluation.py, and evaluation.py is only a command-line wrapper around
  it. A notebook can call the same function and save the file the same way.

  My recommendation: build it at the end of notebook 04.
  - Why 04: it already runs the full selection for "any failure" live. It would
    also run the four single-part event types (about 50 s each) and save
    results.json itself. Notebooks 05 and 06 come after 04 in reading order, so
    they would just read what 04 wrote.
  - It simplifies 04:
    - section 7, the shortlist for every event type, becomes live instead of
      read from the file;
    - section 8, which checks that the notebook matches the file, can be
      deleted, because the notebook is the source of the file.
  - Cost: 04 goes from about 6 to about 10 minutes, since it now does the
    pipeline's work too.
  - evaluation.py can stay as an optional way to rebuild from the terminal. It
    would call the same function, so there is still only one version of the
    logic.

  One catch: notebook 03 runs before 04. The ML benchmark reads results.json for
  its three rule rows. On a fresh run in order, that file wouldn't exist yet.
  Two fixes:
  - compute those three rule rows live in 03 (about a minute), or
  - move the rules-vs-ML comparison table to 04 or 06.

  I'd compute them live in 03.

  Smaller details:
  - The audits take about 15 seconds, so 05 could also compute them live rather
    than read them, making it fully standalone. Then results.json would mainly
    serve 06.
  - The staleness check in 05 and 06 would change its message to "re-run
    notebook 04" instead of "run evaluation.py".

  This is roughly a half-hour change, plus a full re-run to verify that the
  numbers are identical. Want me to go ahead with notebook 04 building the file

⏺ I've added all five items to 06_writeup.ipynb. It re-ran with no errors.

  1. Why alerts are muted for 24 h after a repair, and whether that's reasonable
  (section 9). For 24 hours after any failure or part replacement, alerts don't
  count and those hours aren't treated as normal. That's about 6% of all hours.
  - Why:
    - the crew is already at the machine, so an alert adds nothing;
    - a just-repaired machine isn't running normally;
    - repairs are logged together with failures, so counting the hours after
      them would leak information.
  - Reasonable, yes:
    - only 1 of 719 failures came within a day of the previous one, so the mute
      almost never hides a real warning;
    - notebook 05's check with 0, 6, 24 and 72 hours of muting shows C09's
      precision only moves from 0.92 to 0.93.
  - Risk to watch: a bad repair that fails again the next day. I suggest a
    remedy in the text.

  2. C09, C08 and C10 in plain English (section 6). There's a table of each
  part's sensor and error codes, then each rule written out with real numbers:
  - C09: the part's 6-hour average sensor reading passes its machine's
    1%-of-normal-running level, and that part's own error code was logged at
    least once in the last 24 hours. For example, voltage above about 192
    (normal about 170) plus an error1.
  - C08: any sensor far from normal, and any error code in the last 48 hours.
  - C10: the part's sensor far from normal, and the part is older than its
    typical age since replacement (about 31–34 days).

  3. How the holdout machines are scored, every filter stated (section 9). One
  table covers which machines and events count (350 of the 363 holdout events),
  what is muted or left out, and how thresholds are set. It also states an
  assumption the notebooks don't make obvious. Per-machine thresholds use each
  machine's own normal hours, and "normal" is defined using that machine's
  failure dates. That's mildly optimistic. The January–June threshold test shows
  C09 still holds up with past-only baselines (F1 0.91 vs 0.93).

  4. H = 0, W = 48 vs H = 12, W = 36 (section 11). It states that 0/48 wins on
  F1 and that 12/36 is the pragmatic deployment choice. The cost is next to
  nothing:
  - F1 drops from 0.945 to 0.936, within the noise;
  - no detector or alert changes;
  - the window still covers the whole precursor;
  - a bigger buffer would fail.

  5. Precedent for 12 hours (also section 11). I was careful about what the
  sources actually say. No standard prescribes 12 hours specifically, and the
  text says so. The case rests on three sources:
  - Reliability-centred maintenance: the warning time minus the time needed to
    respond must be "long enough to be of some use" (Reliabilityweb
    (https://reliabilityweb.com/articles/entry/rcm-on-condition-task-interval-de
    termination)).
  - ISO 13381-1, clause 5.3 ("Setting alert, alarm and trip limits"): alarm
    limits are based on the maintenance lead time required. I confirmed the
    clause exists in the standard's preview
    (https://www.sis.se/api/document/preview/905442/), but the preview stops
    before its text. The lead-time wording comes from published excerpts, so
    it's paraphrased, not quoted.
  - 12-hour shifts are common in round-the-clock plants, partly so that longer
    maintenance jobs finish within one crew's shift (Power Engineering (https://
    www.power-eng.com/business/people/making-the-shift12-hour-vs-8-hour/)). That
    article is from 1997. So 12 hours is framed as "one shift": the crew on
    duty when the alert fires can act before the failure.

※ recap: You're building the Oden predictive-maintenance take-home, and I just finished the write-up notebook: plain-English rules, holdout filters, why repairs are muted, and the 12-hour precedent. Next, decide whether to ship it as a zip or a git repo.

❯ why ignore  warm-up and end of the year? also should per machine thresholds   
  be determined using holdout machine data - seems like data leakage, I woud    
  thik they shoul dbe fitted on dev machines' normal hours only                 

⏺ Good questions. I'll answer both, and for the second I'll measure how much the
  leak actually matters rather than guess.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ 1. Why ignore the warm-up and the end of the year? Both are hours we can't
  score fairly, and both are small.
  - Warm-up (first 24 hours of 2015): the rules use 6 h and 24 h averages and
    24–48 h error counts. In the first day those don't have enough history yet;
    a "24 h average" at 3 a.m. on 1 January would be a 3-hour average. Events
    whose window would start before the data begins can't be scored either. On
    holdout that's 13 of 363 events, all stamped 2 January 2015. Their 48 h
    window would start in 2014, before any data.
  - End of the year (last H + W hours, 48 h = 0.55% of the year): an alert there
    can't be judged. The data stops before we'd know whether a failure
    followed. Counting it as a false alarm would wrongly lower precision, and
    counting it as correct would wrongly raise it. So it's left out
    (right-censoring). It only affects which alerts count toward precision; no
    failures are dropped.

  2. Per-machine thresholds on holdout machines: you're right that this leaks.
  "Normal operation" excludes the hours before each failure. So a holdout
  machine's per-machine line is built using its own failure dates, which in
  deployment we wouldn't know in advance.

  But "fit on dev machines only" isn't possible for a per-machine threshold: a
  machine's own baseline has to come from that machine's own readings. The
  honest options are to build it without failure labels, or from past data only.
  I measured both on the holdout machines:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────┬───────┬───────┐
  │                    Holdout F1                    │  C09  │  C08  │  C10  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ Current: own normal hours (uses its failure      │ 0.942 │ 0.761 │ 0.561 │
  │ dates)                                           │       │       │       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ Label-free: all of the machine's own hours, no   │ 0.918 │ 0.764 │ 0.566 │
  │ failure dates                                    │       │       │       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ Past-only: own Jan–Jun hours, scored Jul–Dec (vs │ 0.911 │ 0.720 │ 0.542 │
  │  0.917 / 0.717 / 0.533 currently on Jul–Dec)     │       │       │       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────┴───────┴───────┘

  - The leak is real but small, and only C09 is affected. C09 drops by about
    0.02 (precision 92% → 93%, coverage 96% → 91%), because pre-failure hours
    now pull its P99 line up a little. C08 and C10 don't move, because their
    "outlier-proof" median/MAD lines ignore those hours anyway.
  - Past-only agrees: on the same July–December period it costs C09 only 0.006.
  - Nothing else changes: the ranking, the shortlist and every conclusion stay
    the same.

  My recommendation: switch holdout scoring to the label-free version. A holdout
  machine's per-machine line would come from all of its own hours after
  warm-up, with no failure dates. Dev machines keep using normal hours, since
  that's where rules are tuned and labels are allowed. Every reported number
  would then be leak-free:
  - C09 would become F1 0.92 (precision 93%, coverage 91%);
  - C08 and C10 would stay essentially unchanged.

  This touches a lot of places: one change in src/, then re-run notebooks 01–06
  and rebuild the site. Every C09 number would need updating, in the write-up,
  README, rule explorer and the H = 12 table, plus a new row in the
  holdout-filters table explaining the fix. Want me to go ahead?

⏺ You're reading the page correctly. It shows C09 at 0.942 → 0.930 going from
  0/48 to 6/42, a drop of about 0.01. But that page was built before the
  label-free fix you approved. It still uses the old per-machine thresholds,
  which leak each holdout machine's failure dates.

  With the label-free thresholds, the same rules drop more at 6/42:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┬───────┬───────┬───────┐
  │    C09 holdout F1, rules as kept     │ 0/48  │ 6/42  │ drop  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ live page now (leaky thresholds)     │ 0.942 │ 0.930 │ 0.012 │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────┼───────┼───────┼───────┤
  │ label-free (what we're switching to) │ 0.918 │ 0.889 │ 0.029 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┴───────┴───────┴───────┘

  So "stick with the rules as is at 6/42" was right under the old numbers; under
  the honest numbers it costs about 0.03. The extra drop happens because the
  label-free line sits a little higher. C09 then fires slightly later, and more
  failures (19 of 318) get their first alert only in the final 6 hours, which a
  6-hour buffer stops counting. C08 and C10 are essentially unaffected at 6/42
  either way (0.764 → 0.761 and 0.566 → 0.566).

  Whether a 0.03 drop counts as "small enough to keep the rules as is" is your
  call. It's slightly above the ±0.02 noise level. The rebuilt rule explorer,
  which is generating now, will show these label-free numbers once it's
  published.

      349      return out

      352 +def retune_for_window(panel: Panel, cfg: Config, bank: Feature
          +Bank, dev, hold, kept: list, H: int, W: int) -> dict:         
      353 +    """Re-tune the kept rules for a different window (e.g. the
          + deployment window H=12, W=36).                               
      354 +                                                              
      355 +    For each kept rule: the variant with the best F1 on DEV ma
          +chines at (H, W), chosen exactly as in the                    
      356 +    selection protocol. Then, on HOLDOUT machines at (H, W): t
          +he rule as kept (tuned for cfg's window) next to              
      357 +    the re-tuned rule, with 95% cluster-bootstrap intervals. `
          +kept` is the "concepts" rows of run_event_kind.               
      358 +    """                                                       
      359 +    rng = np.random.default_rng(cfg.seed)                     
      360 +    mk = build_masks(panel, cfg.with_(H=H, W=W))              
      361 +    concepts = {c.cid: c for c in build_concepts(cfg.percentil
          +es, cfg.event, MAPPING)}                                      
      362 +    out = {}                                                  
      363 +    for r in kept:                                            
      364 +        variants = concepts[r["cid"]].variants                
      365 +        as_kept = next(v for v in variants if v.variant == r["
          +variant"])                                                    
      366 +        retuned = max(variants, key=lambda v: summarize(machin
          +e_stats(fire(v, bank, mk.normal, dev), mk)[dev])["f1"])       
      367 +        row = dict(cid=r["cid"], kept_variant=as_kept.variant,
          + retuned_variant=retuned.variant,                             
      368 +                   retuned_rule_short=short_rule(retuned))    
      369 +        for name, v in (("as_kept", as_kept), ("retuned", retu
          +ned)):                                                        
      370 +            f = fire(v, bank, mk.normal, dev)                 
      371 +            st = machine_stats(f, mk)                         
      372 +            lt = lead_times(f, mk, hold)                      
      373 +            row[name] = dict(_clean(summarize(st[hold])), f1_c
          +i=bootstrap(st, hold, cfg.n_bootstrap, rng)["f1"],            
      374 +                             lead_median=float(np.median(lt)) 
          +if lt else None)                                              
      375 +        out[r["cid"]] = row                                   
      376 +    return dict(H=H, W=W, rules=out)                          
      377 +                                                              
      378 +

  Your two write-up additions (06, section 9):
  - "Why ignore the warm-up and the end of the year?" The first day has too
    little history for the 6 h and 24 h averages, and 13 of 363 holdout events
    would need data from 2014. Alerts in the last 48 hours can't be judged,
    because the data ends before we'd know whether a failure followed.
  - Label-free thresholds: the per-machine row now says a holdout machine's line
    comes from all its own hours, never its failure dates. That cost C09 about
    0.02 F1 (0.94 → 0.92); C08 and C10 don't change.

  What changed in 06
  - One window throughout.
    - Section 1 states the window and gives C09's scores there: F1 0.94, catches
      99%, 90% of alarms real, about 24 h ahead.
    - "Key terms" defines H and W using 12/36.
    - 4.4 just says the window is H = 12, W = 36.
    - The shortlist table shows the three rules at 12/36: C09 0.94, C08 0.74,
      C10 0.56.

    bullets, the "with C09 re-tuned" phrases, and the leak-cost aside in the
    holdout-filters table.
  - Section 11, main design choices: H = 12 h and W = 36 h, then a short "Why H
    = 12, W = 36":
    - half a day to act;
    - the window still covers the whole 48-hour precursor and the 24-hour error
      code;
    - on the grid it scores 0.936 against 0.948 for the best window anywhere,
      within noise;
    - a bigger buffer fails.

    The 12-hour precedent (net P-F interval, ISO 13381-1 and 12-hour shifts) is
    kept.
  - Rules written out explicitly (section 6, "The three rules, written out"):
    - the shared definitions (6 h average, robust spread) and the part ↔ sensor
      ↔ error-code table;
    - each rule as an exact two-part condition with real-world values. For
      example, C09 is "a part's sensor more than 3.09 robust spreads from the
      machine's median, e.g. voltage above ~190 (normal ~170), and that part's
      own error code logged at least once in the last 24 h". C10 includes the
      age limits (31–34 days).

  "Per-model" lines are used by none of the three rules, so I dropped that
  wording. The old two rows are now one "Alert lines" row that points to the
  explanation.

  New subsection, "How the alert lines are set":
  1. Sensor condition → a line per machine. Machines differ a little in their
     normal levels, so each machine gets its own line: its median reading plus
     3.09 (C09) or 3.72 (C08, C10) robust spreads. The robust spread is how much
     readings normally wobble, and it barely moves when a few hours are
     unusual.
     - Dev machines use their normal-operation hours. That needs their failure
       dates, which is fine on the machines used to build the rules.
     - Holdout machines use all of their own hours. In real use you wouldn't
       know when a machine will fail, so scoring must not use its failure dates
       either. Only ~4% of a machine's hours come just before a failure, so the
       line ends up almost the same.
  2. Error-count and part-age conditions → one fleet-wide line, set from dev
     machines only.

  I also fixed three places that contradicted this:

❯ what is an alart line exactly, define it properly                             

⏺ An alert line is a fixed number that a rule's condition is compared against.
  Every hour the rule computes a quantity, and the condition is "on" when that
  quantity is past the line in the fault direction: above it, or below it for
  rotation. Each rule has two conditions, so two lines; it alerts in hours when
  both are on.

  There are two kinds:
  - Sensor line (one per machine). The quantity is the part's sensor averaged
    over the last 6 hours. The line is that machine's median plus k robust
    spreads (minus, for rotation). The robust spread is 1.4826 × the median
    absolute distance of the readings from their median. k is 3.09 for C09 and
    3.72 for C08 and C10. For example, a machine whose voltage averages 170 with
    a robust spread of 6.5 gets a C09 line of 170 + 3.09 × 6.5 ≈ 190.
  - Count or age line (one for the fleet). The quantity is how many error codes
    were logged in the last 24 h (C09) or 48 h (C08), or how long since the part
    was last replaced (C10). The count line works out to "at least one". The
    age line is the median age in normal running, ~31–34 days.

  Lines are computed once from reference hours and then held fixed. I'll put
  this definition in the write-up:

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've added that definition to the write-up, and it's live.

  In the write-up:
  - Key terms: the old "Threshold" entry is now Alert line (threshold), with the
    definition above.
  - Section 9, "How the alert lines are set":
    - it opens with the same definition, and that a rule alerts only when both
      its conditions are on;
    - the sensor line is given as a formula, median + k × robust spread (minus
      for rotation), with the robust spread defined, what k = 3.09 and 3.72 mean
      (crossed ~0.1% and ~0.01% of the time for bell-shaped readings), and the
      voltage example;
    - the error-count and part-age lines are explained, including why the count
      line becomes "at least one" (its P80 in normal running is 0).

  Text-only change, so there was no re-run. Pushed as Adhvaith (2b2b8b3).

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've added a "Why F1, and not lift or coverage alone" subsection to section 2
  (Key terms) of the write-up, and it's live on the website:

  ▎ Why F1, and not lift or coverage alone
  ▎ Each of the brief's three numbers can be made to look good by a rule that is
  ▎ useless in practice:
  ▎ - Lift alone rewards a rule that almost never fires: one lucky alert just 
  ▎ before a failure gives an enormous lift while missing nearly every failure.
  ▎ - Coverage alone rewards a rule that is always on: it "catches" every 
  ▎ failure, but almost all of its alarms are false, and the crew soon ignores 
  ▎ it.
  ▎ - Precision alone rewards firing only on the most obvious cases, again 
  ▎ missing most failures.
  ▎
  ▎ What an operator needs is both: most failures caught (coverage) and most 
  ▎ alarms real (precision). F1 combines the two as their harmonic mean, which 
  ▎ stays low unless both are high. For example, when the ideas were compared, 
  ▎ "any signal drifts" (C05) caught 99% of failures but only 28% of its alarms 
  ▎ were real, so its F1 is just 0.44, even though a plain average of the two 
  ▎ would flatter it at 0.64. The matched signature (C09) is high on both (90% 
  ▎ of its alarms real, 99% of failures caught) and scores 0.94. Lift is still 
  ▎ checked (each rule must reach at least 2) and reported, but as a sanity 
  ▎ check rather than the target.

  The C05 figures come from the comparison of all ten ideas in notebook 04. The
  C09 figures are its scores at the chosen 12/36 window, matching the rest of
  the write-up. It's written in the write-up's neutral voice; the README's "My
  decisions" section already credits F1 as your choice.

  It was a text-only change, so there was no re-run. Pushed as Adhvaith
  (9266bf0).

❯ can u explain best of 300 pure-noise detectors: dev F1 0.0861, holdout F1     
  0.071 in 4.5, the table is confusing                                          

⏺ Here's what that line means:
  - The test: notebook 05 builds 300 fake rules from pure random noise. They
    have no connection to failures at all.
  - The selection: each fake rule is tuned and picked exactly like the real
    ones, and we keep the one with the best F1 on the dev machines. That "best"
    fake rule scores 0.086, and on the holdout machines 0.071.
  - The point: trying many rules and keeping the winner manufactures almost no
    skill. So C09's 0.94 can't be luck from trying many ideas.

  The 4.5 output is confusing because it prints that raw line next to a separate
  time-drift table with unexplained column names. I'll replace both with one
  plain table: check, result, what it means.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I'll rewrite 4.5 in plain language. Each check gets a one-line explanation,
  and the code produces one clearly labelled table built from notebook 05's
  saved results:

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Section 4.5 is rewritten in plain language and live on the website.

  What the new 4.5 says:
  - Luck from trying many ideas gets a full explanation, as above. It also notes
    that the kept rules beat time-shuffled copies of themselves, which replaces
    the "z ≈ 42–53" jargon.
  - The other traps, one plain line each: leakage through the failure hour,
    counters that leak the future, time drift, different machines, clustered
    failures.

  The table now reads like this:
